LADIES' LETTER

MY DEAR NELLIE, One day this week I saw a beautiful costume which I will try to describe to you. In the dress worn by the lady both the veiling and lining are white. Rows of white baby ribbon encircle the skirt at intervals, and entirely cover the bodice, which has a full vest of white chiffon. Another very nice one was ono where a fine insertion of lace runs upwards in points, towards the waist, in the front of the skirt, while the deep green muslin sailor collar is also frilled with green chiffon and cream lace. To complete the style of the cellar, a scarf of black velvet is knotted in front, the open- ing being filled up with creamy lace and chiffon. A turquoise blue serge is extren.ely effective with its roll collar of embroidered white satin, edged with chinchilla, that opens over a vest of cream chiffon and yellow lace. The coat is terminated with basques that fall over a plain skirt.

A new fashioned visiting gown is one of pale grey satin cut in a new way so that it fits very closely round the hips, and stands cut in flutes round the feet. The tailed coat is of black silk velvet lined with pale grey satin, and the reverm are embroidered with jet. Five choice crystal buttons appear on either side at the waist, and a cravat of real lace of a deep creamy tone is tied round the throat. The toque which completes this elegant costume has a brim of black velvet, with a crystal butterfly ornament in front, and the crown is of silvery-grey -satin, trimmed with some choice ostrich plumes of the same delicate shade,

The polonaise and the tunic overskirt have brought back the fashion of fringes, and most of 'these trimmings show a combination of chenille or beads with the inevitable silk strands. Smart little dinner-coats, to be worn with jewelled and lace skirts, are extremely chic, but they fit quite tightly at the back and sides, and only the front is full.

For a very pretty coat I should advise you to 'have one trimmed with black astrachan, which when worn over black skirts, and a certain amount of braid or braiding enhances the smart effect. The all-round deep basque has not achieved much popularity. A belt can be worn, or the front left loose, and in either way the coat is extremely chic. Half-inch tubular braid is used on this cost, which fastens slightly to the left side, and the stiff storm ooliar is lined with astrachan. The seams require great care in pressinv, as much of the success of a tailor garment depends upon well- pressed seams, level edges, and a flat surface.

Many dressmakers have scallops, tabs, or van. milykes cut at the side of tee front width, and lap theAo over the first gore, fastening each tab or scallop with a fancy button, and the skirt pattern can he obtained in this fashioned if desired. •Tnere is generally a strip of elastic fixed to each cearn to ke.ep the skirt even, and this must be regulated tr, a nieety when on. Three and a-half yards of 48 to 50 inch cloth will make this skirt, or a little less tee a short figure. The edge should be finished with a folded braid laid to project very slightly beyond the cloth, and a silk frill is indis- pensable. The front, you will find, is very narrow at the top, and niquires to be fitted to the figure, as, indeed, du all Lite seams at the top, for no skirt cell I,soiv set well unless fitted as carefully as a bodice, and the seams ehould be tacked up and fitted before being stitched. Cloth can be used either up or ,lown, and this is intended for the skirt. The first gores join on to the front, an .t the Bide which goes to the front is laid to the teivedge of the material. The back gores are eleped in a straighter fashion, and as these gores on to a sloped side of the back, there is a great deal of spring here, although the top is kept perfectly tight. To those who admire the straight, close skirts, this fashion seems strange and un- common, hut it is now the fashion to have all the fulness at the edge of the skirt, hanging in flutes which sway and flop as the wearer moves. As to whae you said regarding toques last week I may say Parisians wear their toques very for- ward, but the Eeglishwoman generally shows a good deal of front hair. In an instance which I noticed the other day the bows were of heliotrope velvet, carried round to the side in folds, and fastened down with a paste brooch. Two bouquets of Parma violets were connected by the twisted stalks, and pinned down be a large steel buckle. Your loving JENNIE.

POACHED EGGS WITH TOMATO SAUCE.—Stew thoroughly one cupful of tomatoes, add a liberal seasoning of salt, pepper, and a tablespoonful of butter. Trim off all the crusts from the bread and toast daintily, buttering generously. Place a neatly-poached egg oil each slice of hot toast, and pour around the sauce. The toast may be gar- nished with celery leaves. BOSTON CREAMS.—A few waeks since a reader of your paper was asking for a recipe for "Boston cream." Not having seen a reply yet I send a recipe. Three lb. of white sugar,3 oz. of tartaric acid, 2 quarts of boiling water. Dissolve the sugar and acid in the boiling water, and, when cold, add 30 drops of essence of lemon and two eggs.

"WOMEN'S CHIT."

If only intending visitor' t Windsor Castle, would make sure beforehand that the State apartments are open to the public, considerable disappointment would frequently be avoided. During the time the Court i in residence, no vbitors are admitted. A glance at the Court Circular is all that is necessary. Last yeartmore than 100,000 visitors were admitted to the State apart- ments at Windeor Castle.

The " tapestry zooms" at Windsor, which are always occupied by the Empress Frederick, when on a visit to Her Majesty, are all small odd in shape, and anything but light. The tapestry, from which this suite takes its name, is confined to the sitting room, which le lighted by an oriel window at one end. The hangings are in feur panels, two on the same side as the grey marble mantle piece, and two opposite. They are set above a rather high and heavily ornamented dado. Portraits of the late Czar Alexandra and his Czarina fl 1 the empty places on either side of the window, but being back to the light they are difficult to see. There are no " old maids " nowadays, this designa.- tien has become obsolete. And there will soon be no dowagers either. Numters of women, who in days of yore would have been known by this title, no longer uee it on their visiting cards, and in various other ways delicately convey to their friends and acquaintoncea, their objection to the word. The Empress Frederick was one of the first to resent the term, and insisted on taking the name of her dead husband as the definition to distinguieh her from the wife of her son. The widowed Czarina, sister of our Princess of Wales, is always styled "Empress Maria Feodorowna„' she like- wise objecting to the title of Empress Dowager. A few years ago, French was the exclusive language of the Russian Court, now English is very generally spoken. The private apartments in the different palacts too, have a very English look, and this is carried out even to musical instruments, for there is a piano in the mperial apartments by a British firm, and others by the same maker in the summer palaces. Both the Emperor and Empiess had been much in our Isles, and hd admired the arrangements of our households. The Imperial Russian Palace at Czarkoe Skoe, is the rargest thing of the kind in existence. It stands in grounds which are eighteen miles in circumference. When the Czar is residing there it is guarded by 7,000 rldieis and policeman,and it contains 1,000 roams. One room isiwalled with lapis lazuli, another with mother-e'- read, another with amber. The banquetting hall is walled and celled with gold. The Imperial stables are at Peterhof. This delightful palace may be called the Russian Windsor. Its distance from the capital, its quiet sylvan surroundings, and the uses to which it is put all suggest the quoin: old town, on the Thames. The Im- perial stud centains no fewer than 900 horses. Names are given to the animals by the stall master,and it is in- teresting to find that British names are regarded with much favour.

Some horse names may almost be called generie— Dobbin, for example, is as common a name for a cart horse, as Dash for a dog. Carthorses generally have names suggestive of their steady-going habits; What- ever "Dobbin" may mean, it at least sounds highly appropriate to the kind of animal which usually lasers the name. In rural district, it is not an uncommon practice to dub members of a carthorse team with the names of Colonel, Major, and Captain a quaint custom which is paralle'led abroad. The custom of sending valentines is, after all, a pretty one, ard it is a pity that it has almost died out. It is also a matter for regret that our bast valentines should he" made in Germany. but such is positively the case. Somehow British manufacturers cannot tura out a good article at German prices, and of course the shopkeeper, just like his customers, goes to the cheapest market. There used to be a supposition that the first person of the opposite sex seen on Valentine's morn, would be the life partner, but it is little regarded now, even by the most unenlightened. If it was indeed true, the run on milkmen and postmen woold be gigantic. The recent storms and tempests have made it very clear that we have not yet mastered the art of evolving and donning garments suitable for a British winter. The de- sirable covering for women whose business takes them abroad in a gale, is some strong, warm, sturdy garment, of the coat tribe. This offers no unneceasary purchase to the wind. In the majority of cases one notices that skirts are far too long, capes the favourite covering, and that hats are too profusely trimmed. The ideal costume is a skirt that well clears the ground, a low—and save fora neat band of ribbcn—untrimmed hat, or better still a togue or turban, and a neatly fitting coat, which boasts several pockets. A cape in a gale i 4 cold, and cumbersome. Most of its time is usually spent in whirl- ing round the wearers head, which to say the least, is a state of affairs scarcely conducive to her well-being or good temper. As to whether the prevalence of black is due to the order for general mourning, or Lent, it is difficult Fe determine, but in all probability it is to the Lenton season that we owe our present sombre colors. Violet and heliotrope are well worn, Uut black and white are distinctly first favourites at the moment. To those whose complexions are not so fresh as they once were, this combination is often a very tryine one. Heliotre provide I the right shade is selected is infinitely more 13' - coming to those who have passed their youth, as are also bright tones of red. _

If a 'oman is willing to bear the discomfort and ex- pense she can now, it seems, get rid of any wrinkles she may be possessed, and appear with a perfectly new skin. The process is a long P ne, and consists of remov- ing the outer skin by means of acids, when a perfectly smooth unblemished surface makes its appearance. The result sounds deliehtful, but, possibly the majoritv of us would pref., rto keep our wrinkles, rattier than undergo the process.

The Rest Cureis much patronised by fashionable women and not only do they Nurvive it, but appra- the better for it. One goes to bed for two or three orek4,and is fed several times a day. No br,olcs, newsplp,rs,or even let- ters. are allowed. ar-d one's friends are as riaidly ex• eluded as if on' lind the planue. The curl was fir,t started in A Inc rica, by Dr. Weir MitchPll, a fashionable doctor.

We should di well to bear in mind that lettuce. asparagus, watercress. spinach and tematoss, are all wholesome, and likewise valuable ads in attaining a gra-,11 skin and a clear colour. No dinner hould he con- silered complete without a green salad. A complete b tth at least once a day is at siltitely necessary to a good comp`exion, while the face shou'd be nightly subjected to a soap bath, followed by steaming. Plenty of exer- cise, indoors and out, is another phint to remember, even in the larguig pring days. A fair skin i. not to re obtained without cost, and the woman who aims at the pos.ession of an ideal cimplexion, must be willing to make a U.w sacrifices to comraqs it. TOMATPFS BAKED WITH BREADCRUMBS.—SkiII some fine tomatoes. Butter a baking dish, put a thin layer of breadcrumba in the bottom, then a layer of tomatoes, sprinkle with pepper and salt, and strew plenty of bread- crumbs over. Arrange a few knobs of butter here and tire, then put another layer of tomatoes, and cover with hreaPcnimbs and butter as before. Place in a hot oven, and bake an hour.

He asked to be informed of the County Council's action in the matter and had received the following reply "County Offices, Preston, 10th February, 1899. Dear Sir. In roply to your letter of the 9thinstant,the County Council at their meeting of the 2n4 lust., passed a re- solution authorising an application ti be made to the Board of Agriculture to rescind the Muzzling Order now in force in the greater part of the country. The appii- cation has been duly f3rwarded to the Beard, and a reply has been received that in consequence of an out- break of rabies at Blackpool last Autumn, the president is unable at present to withdraw the Order.—Yours truly J. P. Muspratt, deputy clerk of the County Council. Mr. Thompson : I thiQk it a pity that an outbreak of rabies at Blackpool should be the means of the Presi- dent of tee Board of Agrculteire refusing to rescind the order in the township. BE REPAIR OF FOOTPATHS.

The Chairman said there were 25 footpaths of greater or lesser extent running through farmers' fields ss ichin the district, and of the 26 farm, ra there'were only who had nct footpaths running through their land. As long as he (the clairman) could remember the farmers had repaired the footpaths and he considered it was a hardship that they should be called upon to do so. See- ing that the public need the fcopaths he thJught it only r:ght that the District Council or the Highway Board shnuld be asked to repair them, Mr. Thompson siid he considered that the Highway Board had treated Great Sankey very badly in the matter of repairing the footpaths on the main road. Recently pedestrians had been obliged to wade through mud when traversing the foothpath along the main road,

Mr.Halton slid that was a question for the Main Roads Committee of the County Council. With reference to the foctpaths thr-m2h fields the Highway Board would not interfere with them as it was a matter for the land- owners to keep them in repair. It would be establiih- ing a precedent if the Highway Board were to under- take the repair of footpaths. Mr. Thompsm sold he could understand landowners being called upon to repair the footpaths but not the tenants:. He thcught it was a hardship upon them. . .

The Chairman ;aid he had known. cases where the Warrington Highway Board had repaired bridges con- nected with tridle footpaths. Mr. Halton said he was asked by the Council to call the attention of the Highway Board to an unfenced pit at Great Sankey. He did so and was informed that the Highway Board had ao power to do such work. Mr. Thompson said he was of opinion that the High- way Board ought to call upon landlords to repair the footpaths. He did not think the farmers should be asked to do so.

Mr. Halton proposed that the Clerk write to the land- owners in the township asking th(m t 3 to good enough to place the footpath in a state of repair. Mr. Thompsoa seconded, and the le3olution was carried.

Mr. 'Thompson then moved that the Highway Board 1)1 asked to attend to the repair of footpaths on the main roads.

Mr. Taylor seconded, and it was agreed t 3. Mr. Balton said the matter was under consideration and would no doubt be attended to.

THE EX OTINER FRIDAY, FEBRUARY 17 1899

Of implying mat tne hiss was an insult whin must be eradicated at once. Her words also implied that she was very much displeased. But Lord Alfred knew her better than she knew herself, instead of being duly repentant and apologetic. he actually repeated his offence, selecting the other cheek this time. Yet, although this insult, being much more deliberate, might have been supposed to be a proportionately reprehensible one, it seemed to carry its own pardon with it, for the strongest remonstrance it evoked were the words "You really shouldn't." "I really should," was the delinquent's auda- cious reply, "for I love you with all my heart, and I have come here this afternoon solely for the purpose of asking you to make me happy by marrying me." "But that is no excuse for kissing me before you have asked me," was Polly's demurely-spokert reply. "And, besides, I am afraid it isn't any use expecting me to say yes." "And why not? Surely, dear, I have not been mistaken in supposing that you do care for me a little ? "

"N —no, you have not been mistaken, for to tell the truth, I really care a great deal for you." "Then tell me where the objection lies. I know that lam not as rich as your father is, but I can honestly swear that your prospective wealth has never entered into my calculations. I love you for yourself alone, and whether you inherit anything or not, we should be well enough off to live in luxury." "Don't think that of me," exclaimed "'oily eagerly. "We have none of us imagined for a minute that you were anything but a true-hearted, honourable gentleman, and both my father and my mother like you very much. But they like Prince Carlos of Mitylene quite as well as they like you, and he has been here this morning to ask me to marry him. My mother is the kindest mother any girl could have, but she has her ambitions, and she has become strangely anxipas for me to marry the Prince. Of course, I have known for some time that she coveted such a high destiny for me, and the attentions of Prince Carlos have been such as to encourage her hopes in that direction." Lord Alfred listened to this information with constantly growing amazement, for he had reasons of his own for knowing that Prince Carlos was supposed to be very much in love with the lively Lady Flora, to whom he had paid unremitting attention during the past few weeks. "The .fellow is evidently playing some deep, underhand game," he thought, "and I should like to know what he means by paying court to two girls at the same time. Luckily, neither of them return his professed love, but that doesn't do away with the fact that his carefully divided attentions are an insult to both Flora and Polly. I must enlighten my sister a little when I go home." "Then I am to understand the situation to be as follows," he said, taking a seat beside the unresisting Polly, and holding both her trembling hands between his own: 'You are sought in marriage by Prince Carlos of Mitylene, and by the much more humble individual, Lord Alfred Vavasour-Pomeroy, and you are to chose which of them you will accept for your husband." "No," murmured Polly dejectedly, while her bonnie brown eyes filled with tears again. "That is where the trouble comes in. My mother insists that I shall marry Prince Carlos, although I have told her quite plainly that I would rather—would rather—"

"Would rather marry me! Is that it, Polly?" "Yes, that was what I said."

"Weil, you shall marry me, Polly, in spite of princely covetousness and motherly ambition, and now that lam sure of your love, I defy anybody to separate us! " Just for a moment or two Polly gave herself to the happiness which we all feel when we know our heart's affection to be reciprocgted. Then the bugbear of Prince Carlos's suit thrust itself upon her notice again, and she withdrew herself uneasily from Lord Alfred's embrace.

"I am afraid mother will be very angry," she said, "for she told me not an hour ago that she would never forgive me if I refused to marry Prince Carlos."

"But I thought she liked me well enough to be willing to become my mother-in-law," was Lord Alfred's puzzled remark. "She has always been very kind to me, and I have never come to the house without being made to feel that I was more than welcome."

"That is what I can't understand," interrupted Polly. "Only yesterday she said that you were the nicest young man she knew, and that you had been an untold comfort to us lately. Yet this afternoon there is a complete change; and unless she has altered very much again within the last hour, you will find that she is not inclined to be too polite to you." "Did you hear all that Prince Carlos said to your mother?

"No, I did not; but the Duke of Hapsburg Solltesein did, for he came with his friend, and remained with him during the interview with my mother. Indeed, I verily believe that the Prince would have had him in the room while he proposed to me, if I had not been so long in coming down to the drawing-room after my mother came and told me that I was wanted there. I was on the stairs when the Duke came out of the drawing-room, but had not turned the landing, where I could be seen by him, and I gave him time to take his de- parture before I shewed myself. Being thus out of sight, but within earshot, I was able to distinguish every word that the Duke said in an undertone to his friend: This affair must be brought to a head, Carlos, for expenses are too heavy to permit of child's play, and it is too late in the day to risk failure by allowing romance to upset our calcula- tions. I shall see your sister presently, and I shall tell her that everything is settled. I have done my share of the work; you must manage the rest by yourself. ' " "And what was the Prince's reply ? " "He did say something, but I couldn't hear what it was. When at last I. entered the drawing-room, he talked and behaved as if I were the only individual who had ever been in his thoughts for weeks."

Lord Alfred heard this with increasing uneasi- ness, for there could be no doubt that there was some suspicious sort of collusion between the Duke of Hapsburg Solltesein and Prince Carlos of Mitylene. The readiest explanation which offered itself was that the Prince was in love with someone, possibly Lady Flora, who would only have a sm,lll dowry compared with that of the Gold King's heiress, and that there was some mysterious community of interest between the Duke, the Prince, and an un- known lady who was spoken of as the Prince's sister. Contrary to popular belief, it would thus appear that His Highness had a near relative in London; and the thought suggested itself to Lord Alfred that the amalgamation of interests was a sinister one.

The reader will, perhaps, find nothing surprising in this suspicion, but Lord Alfred had had no previous reason for supposing that the Prince and the Duke were more than ordinary society friends; and he had always shared the general opinion that they were both fabulously rich. The fragment of conversation which Polly had overheard was a veritable enlightenment to him, but he was some- what bewildered by Mrs. Rizzen-Quicke's subsequent behaviour to him. That usually considerate and kind-hearted lady told him plainly that Polly would not be allowed to marry him, as she was already promised to the Prince of Mitylene ; and that if he was unmanly enough to try and ruin the girl's brilliant prospects by pressing his undesired suit upon her, he would be asked to discontinue his visits to the Rizzen-Quicke As for the masculine head of that family, he seemed to be resting under a mental cloud, the combined result of his troubles and of his accident; and it was useless just yet to expect him to con- trovert any arrangement which his wife might chose to make.

It will thus be seen that poor Polly, in spite of the joy which Lord Alfred's love for her inspired, was far removed from a condition of unalloyed happiness. For both her father and her mother were strangely unlike themselves; her brother had disappeared, whither nobody seemed to be able to find out; and she was surrounded by an atmosphere of mystery which could not fail to have a depressing influence upon her spirits. Prince Carlos visited her constantly; and her mother was so vigilant in his interests that she found it some- what difficult to avoid him. Perhaps this would not have troubled her much, but for the attitude which he adopted towards her. He seemed to take it for granted that he and she were to be married soon; and he treated her numerous protestations to the contrary as amusing jests, which had no foundation of truth in them.

On one occasion, about four in the afternoon, he brought his friend, the Duke of Hapsburg Solltesein, with him, and both gentlemen were ushered into the drawing-room, where they found Mr. and Mrs. Quicke and Polly indulging in melancholy reflections concerning the probable fate of Teddy. Polly no sooner saw the two gentlemen enter the apartment than she rebelled against their presence, and she actually rose to z. idre her escape. But.

much to her O4ii stomshrnent, tier steps were arrested midway; and, as if in obedience to some expressly-uttered command, she sat down on a settee beside her mother, just opposite the seat of /rich the Duke took possession.

"What can be the matter with me 7 " she askeo herself. "Am I mistress of my own actions, or am I not? I meant to avoid Prince Carlos, and yet here lam actually smiling at him ! I must either be mad or possessed! And what a curious sensa- tion I feel! It is just like cold water trickling down my back, and I am trembling all over. Why, good Heavens! I am surely in a state of mental. and physical subjection!" At this moment, as if irresistibly attracted by the magnetism of the Duke's eyes, she raised her own to them, and became instantly possessed by an idea which was none the less belief-compelling because it was wild and fantastic.

"God help me!" she thought. "Satan himself is looking from those eyes! And I believe the man is trying to hypnotise me! I have heard of such things, and that is what must have happened to father when he did such strange things the other day. But I defy him to hypnotise me—now that I have once resolved that it shall not be done!

CHAPTER XII.. ANOTHER MYSTERY.

Although Polly had braced both body and mind into h, tremendous effort of resistance to what she believed to be a diabolically uncanny influence; and although her sudden alteration of manner ahewed that she was rebelling against that influence, she spoke no word, either to excuse her apparent rudeness, or to explain its cause, but positively ran from the room, leaving her parents dominated by varying degrees of disapproval. Mr. Itizzen-Quicke looked after her, and remarked sympathetically "Poor Polly seems to be out of sorts, and no wonder, either; but I hope she isn't going to be really ill. " Mrs. Rizzen-Quicke did not take the matter so quietly, but shewed herself decidedly angry, and there could not be the slightest doubt that she, at least, would do her best to favour the wishes of Prince Carlos and his friend, the Duke. So pleasant was she, indeed, and so well did her husband now second her promise that Polly should be reduced to reason before very long, and that the wedding s ,ld be an accomplisl ' fact within a month, that the Prince himself soon recovered the equanimity which had been a little upset by Polly's abrupt departure. But the Duke was not BO easily pacified, and he bit his under-lip in the effort to avoid letraying his real feelings. "I will make her repent this," he thought. "And a girl of twenty shall not defy me with impunity. She is not the first one who has become conscious of the influence in time to resist it, but constant droppinc, can wear away a stone, and I swear that she shall feel my power in a manner she little dreams of."

The central object of the visit having elected to take an unceremonious leave of the visitors, and having, moreover, declined to return to them even after she had received a peremptory message from her mother to that effect, the said visitors speedily departed, having first arranged to look in during the course of that same evening. Meanwhile, Polly felt herself unable to rest quietly under the burden of the strange suspicion which beset her, and she soon shewed her ability to act independently and to the point. Rushing to her own room, she donned a hat, veil, and gloves, and, barely waiting to explain to her maid that she preferred to go out without attendance, she hurried off to the nearest post-office, and sent a telegram to Lord Alfred Vavasour-Pomeroy. This telegram ran as follows: "If possible, join me immediately in vestibule of Royal Academy. Vitally important.—Pottv." Lord Alfred had said that he would be at his club, the Junior Carlton, from four to five o'clock that afternoon, so she addressed the telegram to that place, and quietly walked the short journey to Burlington House, feeling confident that her lover would be able to join her in half an hour. Nor was she disappointed. The telegram reached Lord Alfred a few minutes before five, and he obeyed the summons so promptly that he drove into the courtyard of Burlington House before the clock of St. James's had chimed the quarter-past. Then, dismissing his hansom, he immediately ioined Polly, and repaired with her to one of the rooms which were filled entirely with works by the Royal Academicians themselves, and which was, in Lord Alfred's opinion, likely to have fewer frequenters than the rooms which were supposed to be of minor importance. He noted Polly's pale cheeks with anxiety. But he was careful to secure her a comfortable seat before he questioned her as to her reasons for sending him the imperative telegram which he had received. Nor did he neglect the lover's privilege of an affectionte embrace before he permitted more mundane matters to obtrude themselves._

"Now then, dearest," he said, "tell me all about it. You want to consult me about something of vital impostance, and I confess that it does not reassure me to be told to meet you here, instead of in your own home. I hope you are not suffering grave unpleasantness on my account ? " "Nothing more grave than usual, Alfred," was the reply, "so far as my father and mother are concerned, but I have undergone such an odd experience this afternoon that I felt I must tell you about it at once. You know the Duke of Hapsburg Bolltesein ? "

"Yes," acquiesced Lord Alfred, who had just time to wonder whether his Dukeship was turning out to be another Romeo in the field against him, when Polly continued: "And you know that Prince Carlos and he are friends ? "

------- - "Sol have gathered, from what you told me 4fore."

"Well, you will not be surprised to hear that they have paid several visits to our house together, and I have almost unconsciously noticed that both father and mother are more obstinately bent upon making me a Princess whenever the duke has been in their company for a while. But it is only this afternoon that a strange conviction has come to me. Do you believe in hypnotism. Alfred ? " "Most certainly I do; there are too many proofs of the existence of such a power for prolonged dis- belief to be tenable. In fact, nothing but the theory of hypnotism can possibly account for your father's strange conduct on the day when he met with the cab accident; and I have already come to the conclusion that the so-called detective was actually a hypnotist, who had his own vile reasons for subjugating your father's will to his. But what has that to do with the Duke of Hapsburg- Bolltesein ? "

"I believe it has everything to do with him, for I am as sure as I am of my own existence that the Duke Was trying to hypnotise me when I became conscious of some strange influence, and imme- diately determined to resist it. Without waiting a second to deliberate, I -unceremoniously rushed from the room, put on my hat and gloves, wired for you, and here we are." "Thank God that you were able to do all this, for otherwise I tremble to think of what the conse- quences might have been I But one suspicion :gets another, my dearest, and I have already begun to wonder if there is not semr, significant connection between the Duke of HP psburg Solltesein and the man who undoubtedly hypnot sed your father."

"There may be some connection between them," agreed Polly. "But, of course, they cannot both be one and the same man, for the eine is not yet forty, is slender and good-looking, while the other one, so we are told, was stout, elderly, and amply be-whiskered."

"Quite so; but the difference in the two people, great as it may seem, may be more apparent than real, for a man with a deep game to play would not find it very difficult to personate a stout, elderly man, since the whole matter might easily resolve itself into a question of padding and false hair. But now that you have put me on the track, Polly, I will never rest until I have arrived at some solution of the strange things which have been happening lately. Of course, the Scotland Yard detectives are exerting themselves to discover your brother Teddy's whereabouts, but if the earth had swallowed him up he could not have left less trace behind him, and it is not astonishing that success. seems as distant as ever so far as the police are concerned. But lam on a different footing, and it may be that I am destined to put a period to the career of fiendish criminals. At all events, with God's help, I mean to solve the mysteries, and as the whole of my time is at my own disposal, I devote it to working in the interests of you and yours." Polly had such faith in her lover's abilities that she felt sure he would manage to do all he under- took, and she gladly agreed to keep him constantly supplied with 'information concerning every important detail of her home life, so that he might form his own conclusions as to what bearing they had upon their great scheme of discovery. Having arranged to meet at the same place about eleven o'clock on the following morning, the lovers left the building, and had barely emerged into Piccadilly again when their mutual attention was

attracted by some glaring placards contalalll itartling announcements: "Accident to the Princess Delilah of Courla;

"I WOULD RATHER DIE 1"

So wrote a London man jikt before he sent a bullet crashing through his head. On the third floor of art oriinary lodging-house its London, the police found the body of a lodger with a revolver by his bide and a builet in his head. He had been dead over forty-eight hours ; in his pocket was found a book in which was written the falawing : " I am tired of life b.-cause I am suffering fro= Bright's disease. I would try to struggle again and work, but it is impossible TT me to do so, while I ams sick and without the slightet hope of getting better. I would rather die ; bow glad I shall be when I am dead Let the city bury my body." What a commentary on our boasted civilisation ! A poor wretch, suffering from what he supposed to be an incurable disease, snuffs out his flickeriog life with s p'stol bullet. Upon whose head shall fall the blame for his deed--for his life ? Where did he get the idea that his disease was incurable ? Simply from a generally accepted old-time theory,which is still regarded by some as a fact, but which modern investigation has exploded. Many diseases may be considered incurable, but that does not make them so. What we thought to be im- possible ten years ago, is easily accomplished te-day. This is not an age of impossibilities. During the paw few years scientists have discovered that Bright's dis- ease is curable through Warner's Sal. Cure, and simply because tne poor wretch was not aware of this fact, he killed himself. Not so with others. Cures are being daily recorded, alter all other means l•lad failed. The d-atia of tha,, poor, misguided man in the London lodging-house, will not have been without its lesson,if it teaches you that this terrible disease is curable, and that th? remedy—Warner's Safe Cure—can be fouud every. where.

PaINCESS DELILAH.

By MRS. GEORGE CORBETT, Author of "When the Sea Gives up its Dead," "The Prince Physician," "The Vindication of Olive Trevethick," "The Star of Yukon," "The Adventures of an -Ugly Girl," "Deb e May's," "A Fair Blockade Runner," &0..

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: John Mills, part owner of a gold mine and of a most valuable star-sapphire, loses all to the millionaire, Mr. Rizzen-Quicke, at gambling. Rizzen-Quicke gives it him back on the condition that he never gambles again. In gratitude Mills gives the star- sapphire to his benefactor's wife. Meanwhile two aristocratic scoundrels, Prince Carlos of Mitylena and the Duke of Solltesein, are plotting with a stockbroker named Mosenthal to float a bogus gold- makers' company, and incidentally they hope to capture some of the wealth of Rizzen-Quicke and Mills. John Mills mysteriously disappears after withdrawing 4,000 sovereigns from his bank. Teddy Rizzen-Quicke falls in love with the Princess Delilah, who has some mysterious connection with the Prince and the Duke. She promises to marry Teddy if he can secure the star-sapphire for her. It is Mrs. Rizzen-Quicke's ambition that her son, Teddy, should marry the Princess, and her daughter, Polly, Prince Carlos. Teddy disappears in a mysterious way after cashing a large cheque wholly in sovereigns. The star-sapphire also goes. Lord Alfred Vavasour- Pomeroy, who has fallen in love with Polly Rizzen- Quicke, thinks there is something sinister in the disappearances of Mills and of Teddy, and he deter- mines to make inquiries carefully and privately. The millionaire himself is apparently put under the influence of hypnotism, and cashes a cheque in exactly the same way, but a lucky accident prevents his disappearance. Prince Carlos and his friend, the Duke, fall under suspicion.

CHAPTER XL

FOLLY IS REBELLIOUS.

Mrs. Rizzen-Quicke's conviction that her husband had suddenly become insane was modified during the course of another conversation or two with him. So, also, per contra, was his fear that she was losing her senses. Wild as their mutual asser- tions seemed to be, it was soon shewn that they bore a credible foundation, and it became perfectly evident that certain mysterious forces were at work which boded ill to somebody. It needed several people to put their heads together before a cohesive story could be made out of the events which had succeeded the arrival on the scene of Mr. Edgar Sandiman, private detective; and Lord Alfred Vavasour-Pomeroy was as eager as anybody to reduce the chaos of bewilderment to something like definite order.

The more the business was inquired into, the greater was the resulting uneasiness. Mr. Quicke remembered very vividly what took place prior to his interview with the pseudo-detective. He could also give a fair description of the man's appearance, and as this description was corro- borated by two members of the household who had witnessed the arrival and departure of Mr. Edgar Sandiman, it was considered more than likely that the whereabouts of the latter would be discovered before long, unless he proved clever enough to elude the many people who were promptly instructed to find him at any cost. There could be no doubt that his visit was dictated by -a sinister purpose, and in conversation with Detective Raleigh, of Scotland Yard, it transpired that he could not have been recommended by the latter to offer his services to anybody, for he had never heard of him. If, therefore, his errand had not been that which he professed it to have been, what could be the object he had in view? It seemed preposterous to suppose that he had exerted a malignant influence upon Mr. Rizzen- Quicke's mind, yet everything pointed to such a conclusion. Otherwise, how was it that that gentleman's recollections of the previous day's occurrences terminated at the beginning of his conversation with the stranger? And how was it that he had done several things which were so contrary to his usual custom that even he himself was surprised when he was told about what he had done? The inexplicable letter, when carefully perused, shewed itself to be undoubtedly in his own handwriting. It was written upon paper ~.which was never used by anyone but himself, and it bore reference to a subject in which outsiders could hardly be supposed to be interested. Having occasion to sign a few cheques for his wife, Mr. Quicke discovered, by the counterfoil, that he had signed one for Zl,OOO on the very day of Mr. Sandirnan's visit, and inquiries made at the bank by Lord Alfred soon resulted in the further discovery that he had personally withdrawn that amount in gold. No more ominous fact than this could have come to light, and it was with blanched faces that the Rizzen-Quicke family and their good friend, Lord Alfred, regarded each other,. when the latter brought them the information. Their thoughts instantly flew to the still absent Teddy, and they no longer cherised the slightest belief in the reality of the yachting tour. "He has been decoyed away by thieves, or perhaps, murderers ! " wailed the now distracted mother. "And while we have been quietly expecting him to come back, we have been throwing away all chance of saving him! Joe, our boy has been taken from us and we shall never see him again!" It was Joe's nature to be optimistic, but on this occasion he found even the pretence of hopefulness quite impossible, for was there not also the further terribly significant circumstance of John Mills's disappearance to account for ?.And had not both he and Teddy just realised a large amount of money in gold before they disappeared? Besides, there was the further sinister incident of the letter. They all knew that the father had had no intention of leaving home to see John Mills, yet the mother received a letter stating that he was on his way to visit his old friend. How much more credence, therefore, could be attached to the letter which had been received from Teddy under what were, apparently, precisely similar. circumstances? In both cases the letters were evidently intended to lull suspicion until the object of the plotter or plotters had been achieved, and everything went to shew that, but for the cab accident, Mr. Rizzen- Quicke himself would, by this time, have vanished as completely as the other two unfortunates had done. On the very face of it, the sending of the letters was a remarkable mode of communication between people who were never in the habit of writing letters to each other, and, even if this had been the case, they VI ould hardly have troubled to send them through the post, instead of handing them to one of the servants for delivery. _ _ _ - - -

The conviction that Teddy had met with foul play being once established, there was an imme- diate hue and cry, and every paper in the kingdom contained accounts of the mysterious disappearance of the son and heir of the Gold King. Had he been anywhere within reach of a British daily paper he could not have failed to become aware of the sensation his absence was creating. Yet he gave no sign, and day after day passed by without any further trace of him being discovered. The distress on Teddy's account served to diminish the anxiety there had been to discover what had become of the stolen star-sapphire, for as yet the idea of connecting the two mysteries was only simmering in the brain of one individual, Lord Alfred, to wit. The casual observer would hardly have given Lord Alfred credit for the depth of thought which he really possessed, and it would have been a revelation to most people to be told that he was an ardent student of scientific phenomena, besides being an earnest admirer of the theories of Gall, Spurzheim, and Lavater. Yet so it was, and it was after a certain conversation between himself and Polly Quicke that he resolved to put his various mental attainments to practical use. He had gone to the Piccadilly Mansion one afternoon and found Polly in tears. Her father was no longer like himself, for the disappearance of his son dwelt upon him like a nightmare, and he was wearing himself to a shadow in his constant efforts to obtain some elucidation of the mysteries by which he was surrounded. Her mother had broken down under the strain of tribulation, and Polly was afraid lest her illness might develop into a fatal one. Polly herself looked pale and pensive, but as sweetly pretty as ever, for she refused to acknowledge the worst by exchanging her delicately- coloured dresses for the sombre garb of mourning. As we have remarked, Lord Alfred found her in tears, with her face buried among the cushions of a couch in the drawing-room, and he felt that it behoved him to enter at once upon ..he role of comforter. The most effectual way that he could think of to begin his' ministrations was to step softly up to Polly, gently raise her head so as to bring her face in line with his own, and plant a warm kiss upon her cheek. "How dare you!" she exclaimed angrily, wiping her handkerchief all over her face, partly to remove the traces of tears, and partly by way

THE LIVERPOOL PANTOMIMES.

Though it ha i been in full swing for nearly two months, pantomime in the city of Liverpool is as popular an attraction as ever. The enterprise of the theatre managers accounts for this in a large measure, for they are continually introducing new features in the prov,rommes and thus prevent the interest of the public from flagging an the least.

===== 0002601_18990217_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
ROYAL COURT TilliTE{E.

Mr. Robert Arthur's pantomime of " Aladdin" at the Royal Court Theatre, is an unqualified success. Judging by the spleudour and richness of its mounting, and the ability and popularity of the artistes who sustain the east, we canxot help thinking that "Aladdin "as here represented forms one of the Lest pantomimes in the provinces. A. noticeable feature of the performance 13 the close- ness with which the original narrative is adhered to. Lsually one loses the thread of the old nursery tale in the maze of variety turns and spectacular ballets which form the mod rn pantomine. But at the Royal Court Theatre the details of Abanazar's plot and Aladdin's wooing of the Princess are accurately followed out. Miss Claire -Romaine takes the title role, and her piquant,boyish interpretation of the part takes the house by storm. Little Tich is the great draw, however, and only those who know him well in other roles can realise how funny he is as Washee Wash, ein " Aladdin." His won- derful agility, diminutiveness, and originality keep the audience in a roar of laughter. Mr, Aastill Alelford takes the part of Abanazar, the magician, and receives deservingly the warm encomima of the audience for his mock-melodramatic acting. Princess Badroulbadour is represented by Milli Jesie Huddlestone, whose charming manner wins, her favours all through the piece. Mr, Frank Wheeler, Mr. J. Dobson, and the Haytors also con- tribute largely to the success of a performance. which is full of fun, gorgeousness, and originality.

===== 0002601_18990217_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
PRINCE (.47 WALES' THEATRE:

At this popular house of entertainment "Dick W hittington "is the piece de resistance, and nowhere in this part of the country will a more enjoyable pantomime be found. Vesta Tilley pourtrays Dick Whittington, and the famous "principal boy" is the life and soul of the piece. Her vivaeity, naivete, and "go " are infectious and impel her coadjutors in the performance to the most spirited efforts. Vesta Tilley acts her part perfectly, and her popularity with the people increases with her every appearance. Dick's inevitable companion,thn cat, is there also, larger than life, and imitated most naturally by Mr. Freddie Farren. Mr. Walter Groves (as Alderman Fitzwarren), Mr. W.T. Thompson (Jack, his apprentice), and Mr. John Humphries (Sarah, his 030 k), are an exceedingly funny trio whose antics and jokes give the laugh- ter-strained sides of the audience no rest whatever. Mr. Humphries is particularly comical in feminine attire, ani sings" Let 'ern all come "with great zest. Miss Elise Co 3k, gallant Dick's sweetheart, acts her part with engaging sweetness, and shares largely in the plaudits bestowed so largely on the pair. Still more fun is made by Messrs. Tennyson and Gorman, while important additions to the attractiveness of the perfornunce come from Miss Broadfoot-Bell, Miss Katie Cohen, and Miss Olive Marston. It is superfluous to dilate upon the beauty of many of the scenes, they are almost be- yond praise, but we cannot refrain from alluding to the richness and brilliance of the last, the Trades Procession and Ballet in the State Rooms of the Guildhall. The company is a very strong one, the pantomime is full of artistic conceptions, while the music and songs are a prominent and moat enjoy- able feature. As a whole "Dick Whittington" at the Prince of Wales Theatre is an admirable production in every respect, and a credit to Mr. Harold B. Nel- son, the proprietor ana manager.

At No. 6, GeneraT-strest, Warrin4ton, resides a work- in?, min who is, perhaps, at the prelent moment one of the happiest mea in tne Lawn. His name i Ge3rge Ryder. He is 26 years of ago, and is a Noun-

Benefit was obtained from the treatment while he r,nnained in hos- pital, but he had n ) sooner re-urned to work than the disease broke cut again with renewcd vigour, and he Lund it necessary to go about with hoth hands, which were very much swollen. wrapped up and enclosed in cotton gloves. Occlsionaily the fl .sh were torn off his hands when he iemoved the bandages, and his condition was pitiable in the extreme. A spscialist was now eon- sulred, but still the pain and trouble confirmed. FREE CHURCH COUNCIL.

ANNUAL MEETING,

A SCENE: ALDERMAN InBERTS LEAVES THE MEETING.

There was a large attendance in Bold-street Wesle • yan schoolroom on Thursday last week on the occa- sion of the annual meeting of the Warrington and District Free Church Council. Mr. C. E. Parker, ot Penketh, occupied the chair and he was accom- panied on the platform by the Revs. S. Gamble Walker (Birkenhead), C. F. Aked (Liverpool), John Yonge. J. S. Hughes, J. F. Niat-the.ws.J. T. Schofield, J. G. Bennett and G. Herb Atte, J. Warnock, Charles Finch, and Mr. E. England. The meeting was °perm I by the singing of a hymn, and prayer wis afterwards offercel by the Rev. John Yonge. In asking Mr. C. E. Parker to take the chair, the Rev. John Yonge congratulated him upon pr.sieiug over the largest meeting they had had of the Warrington Free Church Council. Ho tolk it as an enceuraging sign of interest in their work. (Hear, hear.) The CHAIRMAN then called upoa the Rev. G. Harbottle to read the secretary's report. The SECRETARY said the Council had 22 churches in association with it. Quarterly meetings had been held during the year, and had been fairly well attended. The meetings had had the good , effect of making free churchmen known to e ich other, and had strikingly manifested their essen tial unity. (Hear, hear.) Whilst anxious to keep the free church movement on religious lines laid down by their leader, the Council had also interfered in public matters outside. They took action with good results in the last election of the Guardians for the poor—(hear, hear)—and also in relation to the propcge 1 transfer of the Feathers license to a house in Orford-lane. (Hear, hear.) The following resolution was pissed by the Council " That this Councl representing the free churches of Warrington, earnestly protests against the transfer of the license of the Feathers }awel t a hoase in Orford-lane, on the ground that the re asonable needs of the neighbourhood are already fully met by existing licenses, and that to increase facilities for drinking in that district i 4 against the hest interests of the people." That resolution had its weight along with other things in bringing about the rejection 'of the proposed transfer. (Applause.) There was a gratifying representation of free cherchnen in court on that occasion and a member of the Free Church Council for the first time in Warrington exercise his right as a ratepayer to protest against the transfer to the astonish- ment of some officials wile seemed unacquainted with that provision of the Licensing Act. At the begineing of the year the Council on the initiative of the president issued an address to the inhabitants of Warringtoa call- ing attention to the importance of public worship. The evangelical alliance week of prayer was kept and the week night services at the workhouse had been vigorously carried on. The Council had also done something to spread information on Free Church princi- ples among the people. A circular was issued among Sunday school teachers and me:nbers of Christian Endeavour Societies, Sire., calling their atten- tion to the subject. After referring to the distribution of other literature the report went on to say that the Council had arranged for the delivery of a course of lectures on Free Church principles. The executive had also decided to give all the support in their power to the Czar's peace manifesto. A public meeting in support of that movement would be held in Parr Hall on Mendsy week when Mr.W.T.Stead would speak. (Applause.) Toe Council also intended to organise a great demonstration in April next in commemmoration of the ter-centenary of the birth of Cromwell. (Applause.) By that time they all hoped the Cromwell statue would be on its pedestal. (Applause.) They clearly recognised the 'service rea- dered to the community by the free churchmen in the Council who moved and seconded the resoluti in in favour of accepting that statue. (Hear, hear and ap- plause.) The CHAIRMA.N, who had a cordial receptien, said Mr. W. P. Hartley, of Liverpool had at first conseeted to preside at; that meeting but in consequence of ill-health Ise wiis obliged to cancel his engagement, and conse- quently the committee had to turn to a lesser man. (Laughter, and no, no.) The speak e. made a touching reference to the death of Dr. Berry and said their sympa- thies as free churchmen went out to tie: family and to the friends Dr. Berry hid left ht-hini, and also to the church which he was directly connecte I with and of which he was a striking ornament and sueh a powerful force. They who were members &: the sister churches ' would wish in that meeting to rec)gnis the help and in- spiration which Dr. Berry's life had been to th,,m. It was Dr. Berry who once said that the argumentative age with its noble results was well-nigh over. That was very true. Their forefathers differed in point of creed, doctrine, church government, the administration of Vie sacrament and other things, but he thoui.,ht the questions which divided their forefather; had not to those who were living in the prssent day the same relative proportion. (Hear, hear.) It was rather the points of agreement—those thins which they could all agree about and meet on a common platf rin—which had come to be in their mind of far grsater impertanc 3 than the minor points which had in the past sleeratecl them. (Applause.) The Free Church Council was the embodiment of a great idea and a great thought. The %idea was that the moral force wlfch was in the Churches and which in separate Churches was somewhat shattered, should be gathered together and collectively they should being their moral force to bear on th(T power of evil. (Applause.) The Free Church Council was in its purpose and aim designed to carry out the great pur- pose for which the Lord Jesus Christ was manifested on the earth, and that was to destroy the works of the Devil. They needed to gather all their forces together and stand four-square against every power of evil and bring that moral force to bear upon every stronghold of evil. (Applause).

THE EX A INTFP, FRIDAY. FEBRUARY 17, 1899

===== 0002601_18990217_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PEACE CRUSADE.

, •at ROUSING THE NORTH.

By W. T. STEAD.

The Crusade of Peace has had a splendid week —the best in many respects since the movement was proclaimed at the great meeting in St. James's Hall. London is giving abundant evidence of interest in the crusade, but it is in the provinces that our strength lies. The Far North of England has ever welcomed great ideas. Often it has given a lead to the rest of England on some question of moment. Its population is by nature one of fighters. Centuries of warfare with the tribes over the border have given a pugnacity to the Northern race which is not yet eliminated. The Tyne, which gives life to the whole district, is lined with shipyards in which huge war vessels are being constructed. The fame of the destructive weapons of Armstrong has travelled over the world. Half the population of Newcastle is directly dependent upon the great Elswick shipyard and ordnance works. Yet the meeting at Newcastle was one of the most rousing yet held. Earl Grey was primarily responsible for the fine gathering at the Town Hall in Newcastle. From the first he has thrown himself heart and soul into the Crusade. As soon as he placed himself at the head of the Northern movement its success was assured. He was accompanied on to the platform at the Town Hall by such a collection of public men as has not been seen in Newcastle for years. Old political friends who went different ways when the Liberal party was divided found themselves once more united in a common cause, and more than one former politician who has deserted public life came from his retirement to speak a word OR behalf of peace. If Newcastle was the centre of the Northern movement, Sunderland and Gateshead were barely less to the fore. The audience at Sunderland was a little cold at first, but before the meeting had finished it was splendidly roused. These fine gatherings had been preceded by a local effort at Burnmoor, over which the Earl of Durham pre- sided. He displayed some confusion of mind as to what the Peace Crusade is. At Darlington there was a capital meeting, with the Bishop of Durham on the platform, accompanied by several members of the Pease family, the devotion of which to every good cause is almost part of the history of the North.

Nothing quite like the enthusiasm of Scarborough and the neighbouring towns has yet been experienced. On Saturday and Sunday three huge meetings were held, and at the last it is calculated that there were not less than 3,000 persons present. There was no need to get up steam at any of these gatherings. The whole population seems inspired 'with the love of peace, and in the three meetings there was not a single voice raised up against the support of the Czar's proposals. Yet Scarborough is precisely one of those places of which a pro- fesaional organiser would be afraid. The tone of London on this great question of the arrest of armaments improves week by week. The best meeting that has yet been held in the metropolis was that at St. Martin's Town Hall. The fine room was filled to practically its full capacity, and the response to the speeches as hearty as could be desired. Mr. G. W. E. Russell delivered a speech full of good points. At the very close of the meeting new life was given to the audience by the enthusiastic eloquence of the Rabbi of the West End Synagogue, who, though not on the list of speakers, begged to be allowed to say a word on behalf of the Jews. The Rev. Hugh Price Hughes was tremendously applauded. In short the meeting was in every respect notable beyond any which London has seen since tles onenina of the camnaien. At Westminster we did not do so well "A good meeting for Westminster" is the verdict of those who know the district. There is a world of meaning in the words. Westminster is one of the wealthy divisions of London in which there is the greatest difficulty in obtaining any public display on any question. It has a large business popula- tion, which migrates to the suburbs every night. Consequently, the task of organising a meeting of any kind is heavy. Yet, with no speaker of English reputation announced, the Westminster Town Ball was more than half filled, and those who were present displayed plenty of spirit and a keen appre- ciation of the object of the Czar. Very different to Westminster was the meeting in the Hampstead Vestry Hall. That building does not hold so many persons as that at Westminster, but it was packed to the doors, and the strength and enthusiasm of the audience was unmistakable. The veteran Dr. Newman Hall spoke with dramatic fervour, and referring to a letter from Mr. Brodie Hoare, M.P., in which the latter states) that he regarded the Czar's proposal as altogether impracticable, the great preacher declared that it was necessary even now to teach senators wisdom. If Mr. Hoare was against the Czar, that was not the case with a more distinguished resident in the neighbourhood. Sir Walter Besant wrote saying that if anything practical came of the Czar's proposal he should join the committee in rejoicing. So well has the. organisation of London gone forward that the Committee of the Crusade, which determined to devote all its attention to the Metropolis and to leave the provinces to look after themselves, now feels that London is assured, and that it can give time to the great towns which have not already arranged for meetings. The number of these is now comparatively few. Without any aid from the central office most of the provincial centres have thrown them- selves into the Crusade. Examples of the extent to which the provinces have organised themselves are furnished by the meetings at Darlington and Kidderminster, which were arranged locally, and carried through without the headquarters being aware of the intention to hold meetings. After the provinces will come Scotland. There the Crusade has so far made but little progress. A few meetings have been held, but there is no evidence that the new gospel df peace has been seized with avidity. No fear, however, is felt as to the result when once work is commenced. Scotland will be untrue to all her traditions if she is behind England in her devotion to a movement which makes for the elevation of mankind and for greater brotherhood among the nations. The metropolitan campaign is to be brought to a conclusion by a mass meeting in the Queen's Hall, which will probably be held during the first fortnight of March. This will be a rallying-point for the whole of London before the National Convention is held. The principal speakers will be Mr. John Morley and, I hope, the Bishop of London, who is, of course, chairman of the Crusade. The gathering should be a fitting end to the movement, which has swept across London. An address to the workers of all Continental countries has been drafted and approved by the Labour Committee of the Crusade. This will be placed before a great gathering of London working- men at St. Martin's Town Hall, on February 15th, to which the representatives of organised labour in the metropolis are offering splendid support. Mr. George N. Barnes, the secretary of the Amalgamated Society of Engineers, is to preside. The labour manifesto, signed by more than five hundred leaders of workmen of every shade of opinion, has now been scattered broadcast.

On the Continent the Peace movement is making progress. From England we are giving what aid we can to those who have the real battle to fight, but it is difficult to do this and not to offend foreign populations. Both in Germany and in France, however, local committees are at work, and the reports from the devoted workers who are giving themselves to the movement are full of evidence that progress is being made against every obstaclea

DARWEN WEAVER SAVED FROM CERTAIN DEATH.

A CONSUMPTIVE FAMILY.

It is a sad day for the Lancashire operative, whom disease or failing health overtakes. I have just beard at such a case (wrote a reporter in the Damen News). Mrs Rosanna Dardis, whom I called upon, is a mill operator, twenty-right years of age, and res des at 35, Nelson- street, Darwen, with her husband and two children, the latter aged respectively seven and five. " I must first of all tell you," said Mrs. Dardis, that my mother, brotaer, and sister, were all taken off by consumption, the last to die being my sister, a little over two years ago. Well, directly after, my turn seemed to have come. Gradually my appetite left me, and as day succeeded day so did my strength fail. This steady decline scoot on for six months. lam a weaver, and of course I had to leave my work, and was beginning to feel as though I couldn't last much longer. I had wasted away to skin and bone, and my surviving sista' had to do my housework for me, for I was like a chill ; I could scarcely walk, and was for days at a time in bed. Accompanying my helpless state of weakness was an incessant, racking cough, which shook ma terri- bly. The doctor told me he could do me no good, and I had properly given myself up. My friends thought I was rapidily following my sister. I became so weak that I could with difficulty gst about, when a friend of mine told me to try Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. I said that I was sick and tired of trying things. However, any husband bought two boxes. After I had taken half-a-box I felt as though I were made over again ; when I had taken three boxes I went back to the mill."

They were justifying a d mend upon the grammarian of the future for a new sex definition. (Laughter.) Without that they would have to speak of men, women, and curat-s. (Laughter.) It was the deter- mination of the clerical party to stamp out Nenconformity. The rule of the priest had cursed every nation where the priest had gone. (Hear, beer.) It stood for the freedom of the sours approach to God, without church or creed or catechism, or ritualism or peiest. They knew nothing about special order e. They stood for the freedom of the ministry, of tee whole Church of God and of every man and woman lei it. Nonconformity might not give them a handle to their name and social advantages but it developed men and made nations. (Applause.) Mr. WILLIAM ROBERTS proposed a vote of thanks to the speakers, the Chairman, the choir and its connecter (Mr. Brow-eh). In doing so he sail they had had a very happy and very successful meeting. As had been already remarked they were met there as free churchnitin and he supposed they would have to agree to differ on some minor points. (Hear, hear.) He approved of every- thing that had been said with one excention, and to that he would refer in a very brief way. He should be very sorry indeed to think tit at party politics meant seeking place and power. (Hear, hear.) Some of the greatest men who had lived in this country had sacrificed place and power for what they believed to be right and true, and if he thought that those who pulled the wires of the Liberal party did so for the mere sake of gaining place and power he would wash his hands of political warfare entirely. (Applause.) The Rev. CHARLES FINCH seconded the vte of thanks. Dr. GORNALL said he did not come to that meeting to Cause discord. He-attended it because it was a meeting after his own heart. He noticed the Rev. Gamble Walker had gone to catch a train, and therefore he did not wish to say anything behind his hack. Me would however, say that he dissented frem the remark which inferred- that his political friends were workine for their own ends. He wonted go so far as to say that he believe] the Marquis of Salisbury was a good man, and one to whom the remark would no apply. (Hear, hear, and applause.) The Rev. JOHN YONGE thlllght they had every reason to be gratified with the meeting. It had been in point of numbers, interest, and enthusiasm tee best Free Church rnee:ing Warrington had seen. (Applause) It was a matter to be regretted that even by a THOU com- plete and perfect accident a wrong note was struck by one of the sneakers. He leokel upon him as a brother Christian minister, and felt perfectly sure that nothing was furneer from his intention than to a say a word which would effend the sensibilities of any person pre- sent. (Applause.) He most sincerely hoped that hi 3 friends Alderennn Roberts, Mr. William Roberti, and Dr. Ger len would look upon it in that light. Personally h did not agree with the statement which he understo-el was made, although he did not hear the remerk. as his attention was occupied at the time with semething else. The vote was unanimously carried. The CHAIRMAN ire responding said he was sorry he had to exercise his prerogative and stop Alderman Roberts. The meeting might easily get into confusion if every one who differed from the speaker was to rise whilst he wal speaking and express that difference. Persoreally he dissented from the statement made, and he was in- clined to think the speaker put it a little more strongly than he meant ta do. The incident, however, had occupied a greater place in the proceedings than it ought to have done, and he hoped they would carry away wilh them thee message of the speakers. ( eppleuse.) The Rev. JOHN YONGE thee pronounced the benedic- tion and the meeting terminated.

OUR LOCAL CRURCHES.

LOOKING BACKWARD.

" Leoking backward—A glance at some facts in the history of our race, bearing on the present situation" was the title of the lecture on Sunday in the Christa- delphian Room, by Mr. S. F. Willson, of Stockport. In looking at the present situation with regard to ourselves, Mr. Willson averred that our knowledge of the past was due solely to what had been r.corded, and that of the future we know nothing, excepting what the Bible reveals. They would have beard the observation when a man had (lei " He knows the great secret now," but the Bible says "The dead know not anything." Going back to the time of Christ. he showed how the enemies of Jesus spoke of Him, and what the work done by Him and his disciples consi‘te3 of, i.e., the preaching of the Gospel, or "The Kingdom of God" for the terms were inter. changeable. The disciples uuierstood Christ was to be King of Israel, and to rule the nations ; but they under- stood not that he was to die first, but they preached the Gospel—under Christ's directions—without under- ' standine the latter fact. After showing the reason why it was necessary for Christ to suffer, the lecturer stated that naturally they knew nothing, but from childhood by observation, perception, imitation, school and work I they acquired knowledge, and spiritually they knew 1 norhing apart from revelation and they could only get knowledge by degree from God's text book. Some men I would claim to study Gcd in nature and the results Darwinian fol:owed for Christ could be known by revelation only. Jesus Christ's words were "Whosoever shall not receive the Kingdom of God as a little child.he shall not enter therein." They could not commence with deep theological matters first. They must commence at the rudirnen's or they would resemble a person trying to understand Euclid. before he knew his alphabet. Locking further back to the time of Adam, Mr. Willson examined the entrance of sin into the world, and death by sin. Adam made a way into the grave, and Christ opened the way from the grave. He asked them to cast aside their fancies and theories and take the word in its simplicity as did the Bereans. They could have eternal life by seeking for it, and accepting God's condition,for "God will render to every man accercing to his deeds : to them who by patient continuance in well doing seek for glory and honour and immortality eternal life.

FAREWELL SERMON OF THE REV. A. A. MARKHAM.

On Sunday even'ng lite Rev. A. A. Markham, who has been appointed vicar of St. Jude's Church, Hard- wick-street, Liverpool, to which living he was in- ducted on Thursday evening, preacked his farewell sermon in St. Clement's Mission Church, Bank-street. There was a very large congregation, every seat being ercupied. The preacher selected for his text the words, " Only let your conversation be as it becometh the gos- pel of Christ : that whether I come and see you, or else be absent, I may hear of your affairs, that ye stand fast in one spirit, with one mind striving together for the faith of the Gospel." Philippians i. 27. The rev. gen- Veman remarked that they were, in the town of War- ring, proud of being Lancashire people, and still more of , being Englishmen, but he was sure they realised that it was prouder still to be a citizen of Christ's heavenly , kingdom. They had need in the public life of the Church to" stand fast "in one spirit, when they read the news- payers from day to day, sometimes laden with such slanderous tales, and when they saw that such a delight , was taken in creating crises. There were people who appeared to take a delight in raking up these things and showing them in lights they ought never to bear. They must not be affrighted by those things, as there were two sides to every question. Alluding to tli3 forth- coming departure, Mr. Markham said his three prayers tor them were that their manner of life might be as be- come th the Gospel of Christ, that they might be at one amongst th ,mselves, and that they might have the courage to strive together against all their enemies for the precious faith of the Gospel committed to them. One oked back at the years spent together, and he remem- bered most vividly all the kindnesses he had received frem them, also the times of depression and times of in- terse happiness, when schemes had been successfully carried through. He would say it honestly, but he had learnt from them more than they had learnt from him. Close friendship had arisen, but be did not think they tad finished ; they were too close to be broken by the slight change he saw before him. He also remembered the vast amount of work that had been left undsne, al- th,u2n in the generosity of their hearts they always thr_u?ht s kindly of clergymen ; they were usually re- inerulered, and that wal a comfort. Ha would make one reqwst, and it was a simple orie, namely, that they would not torget him in their prayers,for he would need them vc ry sorely when he got to his strange and large parish. BY MR. WILLIAM EILIS.

THE EXAMINER.

FEBRUARY riM, 299.

IT is generally admitted in Common sense this country that for every wrong ard there is a remedy, and that Parlia. ment, as CORE said, can do everything but change a man into a woman. It is just as generally and wisely conceded that every anomaly and anach- ronism cannot long withstand argument and com- mon sense. Yet we have Mr. BALFOUR, who is nothing if not philosophic, positively saying that we should be "absolutely insane if we sought to fundamentally alter the House of Lords. Th case stands thus. Household suffrage is the basis of political power, and the House of Commons is the place of political initiative. How is the popu- lar Chamber to be harmonised with the House of Lords, in which the overwhelming majority is of hereditary qualification? That is the problem, and it recurs with every change which gives us a Liberal Government. There is thus a danger to be feared and faced. The Lords are" amenable to public opinion" when it happens to be Conservative; they are hostile when it happens to be Liberal. This cannot be indefinitely continued without violent wrenches and much disturbance. Mr. BALFOUR thinks the House of Lords preserves us from "violent floods and ebbs of public opinion.P It does nothing of the kind. There was a big Con- servative reaction in 1874, and an equally big Liberal reaction in 1880. There was a huge Union- ist movement in 1886 and in 1895. The Lords did nothing to create or to check these floods and ebbs. The flood of 1895 has been helped by the Lordsl, It has neither checked Irish land legislation nor compensation to injured workmen, though in the latter case it mutilated a Liberal bill in 1893, and passed a more drastic measure in 1897. The ab- solute lunacy, it seems to us, consists in believing that with increasing democracy the power of the hereditary Chamber can be fully maintained. The House of Lords has little real revisory power. It can throw out a Home Rule Bill, but it has not settled the question involved. It has sanctioned Irish Local Government, and on that basis will be erected the coming Irish Parliament. Indeed, the House in question has its own floods and ebbs, its panics alai its languors. Big social questions touch its members, and their feelings and interests impel them to resist. Hence it is the work of common sense and not of lunacy to provide for an outlet, without such a costly and tumultuous affair as a general election. The Constitution demands this readjustment, and if it be not provided for in quiet times there will be a revolution, and the second Chamber will go. As a matter of fact, the House of Lords is the only unreformed Upper Chamber in Europe, and the Conservatives stand by it from purely party motives, because it mitigates defeat when they are overthrown, and is but mildly remonstra. tive when they enter into large reforming compeit titions. Hence the arguments of Monday, and the acquiescence of the existing political majority. The true wisdom is to avoid these extremes,and it can only be done by providing the means whereby the existing majority can obtain its will in a con- stitutional and an eminently pacific manner. We Are in no way depressed that M a House of Com- mons of three-hundred and twenty-eight memberg two hundred and twenty-three decided against a elan for over-riding the veto of the House of Lords. Such a change is bound to come, or tke House will be swept away. Peers themselves will come to sect during the next century, that this is a safer plan than obstinate resistance and dissolutions. At any rate, the country will have to face the ques- tion at the next election.

TILE expansion, the glory, thThe e Distractions of c3mmercial gains of Empire seem to be just now attract., ing more than ordinary attention. Questions are asked to determine individual attitudes towards the Empire at large. There is an effort to mak* new divisions and to invent new political titles and to treat home affairs as of relatively small ira.4 portance. It is obvious that there is little times though there is more necessity than ever, to cona eider the distractions of Empire. These distracd tions do not lessen, and they are not likely te lessen, because what we call an Empire is a caeca tion of States and dependencies, bound together by no well-defined organisation, and contributing little or nothing to common objects. For many years tolcome,the organisation of this vast Empire will require the best thoughts of the Ind
