There are- boundaries to settle, forts to build, railways to make, ports to improve, tariffs to adjust, commerce to develop, feuds to assuage, and local to harmonise with Im- perial rights, to say nothing of the enormous duty of Imperial defence. We must expect less and less attention to domestic affairs, fewer organic changes, and a certain feverish weakness, due to exhaustion and excess of anxiety. Crofters in Scotland will attact less attention than a revolt in Uganda, the development of Ireland will be subordinate to the populating of the Soudan. Problems of local government will be neglected whilst we discuss municipal reform in Calcutta. The cost of living to the industrial poor at home will be pushed aside to examine the prevention of famine in India. How to develop ' Canada becomes of superior importance to the decline In our cotton and woollen and tinplate industries. How can we seriously deal with a reform of the House of Lords when we are busy over the ruling of Rhodesia, the future of Wei-hai-Wei, the federation of Australia, and the reorganisation of the Empire? What prospect is there of seriously and continuously taking education in hand whilst we are setting up a Gordon College at Khartoum and distracting ourselves with the results of educa- tion in India? We obtain an insight into the working of these Imperial distractions in the .current situation. Her Majesty's Ministers are beset with problems abroad, of almost every kind, and so they are compelled to excuse themselves for a non-heroic sessional programme. The Unionists came into power to give us large social reforms. Their present programme consists of more old bills than new ones. "We must have a shorter session, fewer private members' nights, and days, and more control over public business," say, in effect, the Ministers of the Crown. Why? Because there is much to be done else, and for many months it is pleasant to be relieved of Par- liamentary duties. A. big Empire cannot be die tracted by pettifogging domestic *concerns. Serious discussions are called silly. Ardent Welsh, Scotch, andirish reformers are laughed at. We are getting fonder of the Empire and more neg lectful of Great Britain and Ireland—poor little unimportant places

The news which comes from New York about the weather, telling, as it does, of a state of chill beating the records of forty years, is in strange -contrast with our own experiences,and will hardly be credited by men whose memories of New York are associated with burning heat. The tremendous storms that have swept the Atlantic during the Vast few days may do the steamship companies Considerable harm by strengthening a false impres- sion. The is a belief in some minds that it is Well to pick months for trips across the Atlantic, and that in this way a mere pleasure-seeker can be sure of an easy passage. That, however' is very far from being true. The developments of Of shipbuilding have made the passage practically safe, for it is extremely rare that a big vessel .finally gives way, however much buffeted by the storm. But across the broad Atlantic the chances of a rough or a smooth passage are nearly equal all he year round, and at no particular date can a, ensitive person feel sure of comfort. All the com- panies can give him is confidence, and the newer ships should certainly inspire this. The weakess part of the arrangements necessarily is in the pro- visioning. A large quantity of food is always pro- vided extra for contingencies, but when you think of the value of space, and the number of pas- sengers, you prefer not to ask how many days be- yond the recognised time it would last. There is always a remote possibility that a voyage may be indefinitely prolonged, and food cannot be indefi- nitely provided.

It remains to be seen whether the Glasgow Christian Social Union has not come within reach of the libel laws in publishing a white list of firms who do not sweat or in any other manner injure their workpeople. Our libel laws are precise "'Tiough in their wording, but the interpretations Placed upon them are often surprising, an .1 a firm might complain that the mere fact of its non-ap- pearance in the white list was indirect defamation. A black list, containing the names of business firms who subject their workers to life in insani- tary workrooms, coupled with long hours and sweating pay, is clearly most dangerous, though ,the allegations may be capable of substantiation Till now the public who do not care to purchase sweated goods have had to trust to the humanity of the business men with whom they deal, but time and again the investigations of social workers have shown that the trust has been misplaced and the confidence of the. public abused. The public cannotbe blamed, therefore, if it has become un- duly seems to be no means of find-. -lng.out the truth by •personal effort, and when any society takes upon itself to advise it is confronted by the spectre of libel. Most people are convinced of ..the necessity of putting an end to the unholy ,evils of competition. Only a few are willing to buY sweatedgoods. It offends both their self-res- pect and their conscience, Purchasers have there- fore a right to be protected against unconscious :support of a system which revolts them. The dia. .culty is to find a method.

The Gladstone Memorial Fund seems to have Ireached sufficient dimensions to make ample pro 'Vision for the Institute of St. Deniol, and perhaps ;leave a surplus out of which a statue might be provided. £30,000 is the total sum realised, and this is, in many quarters, considered a national response worthy of that dominant personality, the like of which we, at all events, will probably never see again. Personally we dissent from this view. In our opinion £30,000 is but a poor and insigni- ficant tribute from the millions of people Mr. Gladstone laboured so unceasingly to serve. Only a short time ago £100,030 was subscribed in less than half the period which has elapsed since the death of Mr. Gladstone, and for what? Simply for the erection of an educational institution in far away Khartoum. But this was at the request of a successful warrior, a man whose praises were in every mouth, but a man also who has performed nothimg like the service for his country that Mr. Gladstone did. Of course, it is gratifying to sea that the primary object of the memorial scheme will be carried out, and in a manner which would have gladdened the heart of the man whose memory it perpetuates, but we are, nevertheless .disappointed at the rr.,ngreness of the public -recognition of Mr. Gladstone's greatness and good- ness.

Were it not for the unfortunate incident which Occurred at the annual meeting of the Warrington 'Free Church Council last Thursday evening its sue- rc'eas might have been stated to be complete. There Was a healthy tone about the meeting and the in- creased interest of the public as shown by the large attendance augurs well for the future of the "Council. But we arc afraid most of those who at- tended would carry away with them a feeling of regret at the incident which led to Alderman oberta leaving the meeting. It arose in the most unexpected manner and was a breeze of but a moment's duration. The statement which called it f o th was certainly of an irritating kind. Tile rev. Gamble Walker, of Birkenhead, in the course of his remarks defied any person to prove that "party politics were anything else than fighting for place and fighting for power." This, of course, is the wildest possible nonsense, and it would have been very easy to prove it so. Party politics have ikladoubtedly their blemishes, and there are some black sheep in both folds whose avariciousness is contemptible, but the same remark might apply to the Christian ministry, and yet an indictment against the whole body Would be as absurd as that of the speaker's on Thursday evening. It was there- fore not surprising that the imputation 811culd be resented and the challenge taken up. 'Mit We think that Alderman Roberts would

have been wiser had he exercised a little of that patience which is proverbially a virtue, instead of insisting on being heard there and then. • Had he done so Mr. Roberts would have had an ideal op- portunity for exercising his ability as a political debater and worstipg his opponent. Ttie cudgels were taken up later, we are glad to note, by other speakers who were equally determined not to let the Rev. Gamble Walker's rash remark pass un- challenged. But after all the disagreement is not very serious, and it is to be hoped that it will end where it has begun.

A gratifying feature of the Chief Constable's re- port, which was issued last week, is the marked decrease of serious crime within the Borough during the past year. Taken as a whole Warrington compares most favourably in this re- spect with the majority of towns. In 1897 the number of serious crimes committed was 160, and last year this number fell to 119. If the number continues to decrease in a corresponding ratio Warrington will figure still less prominently at the Liverpool County Sassioos and Assizes, which is a consummation much to be desired. List year 11 persons were committed rn• trial— a low average considering the simi of the town. With reference to the number of persons charged with drunkenness there is not the same ground for congratulation. The number dealt with has increased from 277 to 297 which is the highest number since 1895. Still it is less by one half the number of cases of drunkenness recorded some five years ago. This may be accounted for by the greater leniency now being shown to " quiet " drunkards, and has at least enabled the town to rid itself of the unenviable reputation it held not long since of being the most drunken town in the -United Kingdom.

The annual congregational tea party in connection with All Saints' Mission, Bewsey-road, was held in the schools, Hoyle-street on Monday evening. There was a very good attendance. During the ait,r proceedings an interestiae presentation was made to the Vicar of the Parish (the Rev. H. V. Pigot), who is shortly leaving Warrington to take charge of the Parish of St. Thomas's, Stockton Heath. To show the respect in whicn he is held,the friends at the mission di cided too make a formal presentation to Mr. Pigot, and it took ' the form of a pair of beautiful silver candlesticks. The presentation was made by Mr. Siedlow, one of the churchwardens, who said that that was their ninth annual tea party. In making Mr. Pipt a present, be said the subscribers thought they wouid give him some thing that would be of use to him in the church. He thoueht it was a very wise present. He then presentod Mr. Pigot with the caedlesttks.—Mr. Pigot, havir g thanked Mr Sudlow, said he had no idea whatever ttiee. they were going to give him a testimonial, but he thanked the sulvcribers very much for their kindness. His aesociatlon weth Mr. Brierly had onlyeextended over a period of three and a half years. He thanked Miss Westwell for the valuable services soe had rendered to them; also Mr. Worley and Mr. Lily, the orgenist. It was unnecessary to state who would be their next vicsr. the Rev. E. R. Tarbuck, and he hoped they would con- tinue their work in the same happy manner as if no change had arisen. (Hear, hear.) The Rev. H. Brierly said this was his fourth and last tea party with them, as he was also leaving the parish. He hoped the change would make no difference. Be would be with them a few more Sundays yet.— During the evening the following programme was gene through :—Action song by six boys, "The Jolly Jack Tar "song, Mr.A Grimsha w, " Mary of Argyle" ; Mrs Brierly, ‘‘ The Miller and tee M " ; song, Mr J Pennington, "I want yer ma honey " ; song, Mr R Derbyshire, "Johnny Jones " ; song, Mr Grimshaw, " When °Vier lips" ' • song, Mrs Brierly," My Old Dutch " ; stump speech by Mr R Derbyshire; song and chorus, Miss N Gardner and six children, "Go to By- bye." The entertainment conendei at half-past nine, and then dancing was indulged in until eleven o'clock. On Tuesday evening a presentation was made at St. Clement's Mission to the Rev. A. A. Markham, who was on Thursday inducted to the ilicambency of St. Jule's Liverpool. It consisted of an illuminated album, bound in scar:et neoroceo, containing the names of 200 subscribers, and a solid silver tea and coffee service, one of the pieces of which b)re the fol- lowing inscription :—"Presented to the Rev.A.A.. Mark. ham, M.A., by his friends at St. Clement's Mission,War- rington, on his appointment to the.perpetual curacy of St. Jude's, Liverpool, February 14th, 1899." The service, which stands upon a roseweod tray, was specially provided by Messrs. W. Woods and Son (Alfred Woods), of Sankey-street. Trio large attendance at the gathering on Tuesday even- ing was eloquent testimony of the popularity Mr. Mark- ham has enjoyed. The Rector presided. and was sap- poned by the Revs. A. A. Markham, and G. W. Dicken- son,and Messrs. W. Hodlson,F. J. Whittle. J. Dowhurst S. Davies, and A. Woods. Letters of apology for non- attendance were read from 'dr. Omen, Mr. Craik and Mr. R. Davies. He had been to him something like Timothy was to Sc. Paul, a real son. It was with the feeling of a spiritual father teat he regarded him,and he felt more deeply than he could express what he should lose when he went. No work could exceed that which had been done by Mr. Markham both for the parish and in that mission. kApplause.)2The Rev. A. A. Markham, who was loudly applauded and seemed to be deeply moved by the cordial outburst of, kindly feeling, simply but very eloquently returned his thanks. LaDGER.—This wee A case in which Mrs. Theresa Hughes, of Helsby, wife of Mr. Thomas Trughes of He!sby, claimed £3 3s 3d for board and lodgings and money lent, from Mr. John Gull- foyle, timekeeper, at the Telegraph Works, Helsby, alleged to be due in respect of lodgings and board, due for two weeks in the beginning of January 1898, and for one week in August of the same year.—Mr. J. Burton appearei for the plaintiff, and Mr.H.N.Linaker(Messrs.Linaker and Linaker) defended. —The plaintiff was called, and said that the defendant came to lodge with her on the 15th January 1893. When be came he was penniless, and her husband got him work at the Tele2raphic Works. He did not pay for the first two week's lodgings ; and she did not ask him, for she wanted him to pay his old loieing money in Manchester. She lent him money from time to time, and she had lent him 5s 93 when he returned from his holidays to buy brandy with to make him right to go to his work.—Cross-examined Plaintiff admitted she kept no books or sc2ounts. She had never asked Guilfoyle for the money after he left ; neither did she ask him at the time he went for his helidays in August, because she trusted to his honesty to pay her. There had been robberies at the Telegraphic Works and , Guilfoyle who was an ex-Irish constabulary inspector had traced them to Hughes, and a warrant had been issued for his arrest. No claim had been made until efter the warrant was issued.— The defendant Guilfoyle said he left Manchester where he was in the employ of the Lancashire and Yorkshire Railway Company, as a conptable, to come to the Helsby Telegraphic Works, on account of better pay. He had paid his lodging money regularly—every Saturday. He denied that the plaintiff ever lent him money to buy brandy. and when he left his last lodgings he had paid all. —Mr. Barton failed to shako this witness in cross-ek,..mination.—His Honour said it was a case of oath against oath. and he saw no reason to disbelieve the defendant, and he must therefore give judg- ment for the defendant.

Tna FLOODS AT Dirrox.

Owing to the exceptionally high tide, an extraordin- ary cccurrence was experienced at Ditton, near Widaes on Sunday. Ditton Brook, which empties itself into the river Mersey, overflowed its banks and the surrounding country was completely under water. Close to Ditton Junction Station the land is very low and dozens of cot- tages were flottled,and people had the unpleasant exper- ience of having to wads about their houses. The water spread with great rapidity over the lowlying fields, con- verting them into an enormous lake. Hale-road, Close to Marsh-bridge was flaoded over, and people coming from church were unable to get home until Mr. James Mercer came on the scene with his trap and kindly put it at their disposal. On Monday, at Ditton junction,there was a similar experience. Toe water rusted over the banks at Ditton Brook with great force, ani soon found its way into the houses, and the occupants had to take refuge ill the upstairs portion of their dwellings. Great inconvenience was caused, but happily the damage was not of a serious character, Fortunately, the Landon and North-western Railway, which is close to the brook, is on a slight embanhment, otherwise the damage might have been mote serious. Such an occurrence his net taken place for about ten yeare. OITTEREAR OF FIRE IN HIGH-STREET.

About half-past eleven o'clock on Tuesday morning an alarming outbreak ot fire was discovered on the premises of Messrs Linaker and Linaker (solicitors) High-street. Runcorn. Fortunately, however, the fire was caught in the nick of time, and owing to prompt action on the part of Mr. El. N. Linaktr, and Police-constable Wilson (311), was soon brought under control. It appears that the fire ariginat3d by the over-heating of a flue, from the bakehouse at the b tck of Mr. Mellor's shop, causing the floor of the attic to get on fire. It was discovered by Mr. H. N. Linaker, who upon his return from the County Court, heard a noise as of a portion of one of the upper rooms falling. Upon investigation he found that a fire of considerable dimensions was raging there. At once he raised an a!arrri, but what was better, he promptly took off his coat and securing a bucket of water commenced a single handed fight against toe flames. Assistance soon arrived, Constable Wilson boing first on the scene, and in a short time the fire was got under. The Runcorn Fire Brigade came up in a short time, but were able to control the fire without bringing the hose into play. The extent of the damage is pretty considerable when it is considered that some valuable decutnents were des- troyed. The building is covered by insurance. Thanks are due to Mr. Linaker, P.C. Wilson, Messrs. R. Wright, J. Lea, A. Anderton (New inn), and the members of the Fire Brigade under Superin:endent Wright for their prompt and valuable assistance. PRESENTATION AT RUNCORN.

HALF-HOUR LECTURR,WIDNES, 1 Councillor Laws, presided over a large audience at the leo:ure upon " Lancashire poets," by Mr. A. Lancaster, given in the lecture theatre of the Technical Schools on Friday evening. The Chairman introduced the lecturer as the librarian of the neighbouring town of St. Helens. —Mr. Lancaster, who was received with applause, said that in all ages and countries ballads had been used to commemorate passing events. In Anglo-Saxon times they were employed as a stimulant to the warriors. Later, the ballad enjoyed a great popularity in country districts, and nearly every mansion also had a gallery for the musicians. The programme on the festive oaca- sions comprise 1 billad, interlude, and song, which fol- lowed in quick succession. Since these times nearly every event had blen put in verse by the b monger, but very few of them existed prior to the time of James I. The great charm which many of the Lancashire dialect ballads had was not surpassed by poems writtea in pure litaglish. It was not un:il the days of Collier, more popularly known as Tim Bobbin, that the Lancashire poets began to write to any appre- ciatSe extent. The lecturer went on to say that if a Londoner come and spoke to some of the Lancashire people, he woeld wonder what new language it was,and i if he was told that in that language was written some of the gems of English Petry he would sco t the idea. The lecturer then proceeded Co refer more directly to the Lanc ishire poets, of whom, he said, there were more than 100. Mr. Lancaster read quotations and poems from the works of William Shenstune, Samuel Balmford, , John Bottoo Rogerson, William Hirrison Ainsworth. Ac.' —lt the cenclusion of the lecture, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. Lancaster, upou the motion of the Chairman.

BRUNSWICK MUTUAL IMPROVEMENT CLASS.

Wednesday evening Mr. TimotoySimister pr3sided at the weekly meeting of this ctass. The evenin,4 wa, taken up with a lantern lecture on " Cambridge " given by Mr. T. 4.. Lawrensan, hladmaster of th 3 Rene )rn Technical Schools. The lecture was highly aporeciAt,o by the taro atedienc3 present. Votes of thanks t,-) the lecturer propased by Mr. Jones, and secmded by Mr.B. Crosby brought the proc'e 1i to a close. • CHARACTER, SKETCHE --- BY " Q.E.F.D." It was, perhaps,Dickens and Thakceray who first showed to the British reading public that scenes taken from daily life had an interest and a charm unsurpassed by the wildest dreams of the imagina- tion, and proved inconteAably that the common walks of life held men and women who were true heroes or heroines, though comparatively unknown out..side their own little sphere. Each town and hamlet bears witness to the fact that there are per- sona beating out life's music in solitude, unheeded and unknown, who live self-denying lives; who en- deavour to raise the standard of morality, and seek to bring a ray of light into the lives of their more unfortunate fellows. There are, again, _those persons full of energy and "push" without whom it would be impossible for even our smaller towns to be carried on without finding themselves in financial difficulties. Men clear-headed,farsighted, prompt t 3 act when the occasion arises; men born to rule and command and lead the van in every enterprise of worth and m3vement. There are, yet again those who are the lees and dregs of society(?) and who are seen in every small community. Tile loafer ready to beg, borrow or steal the necessaries of life; the man in the street ready to earn an hon- est copper by the "sweat of his brow," and, worse than all, that class whom even the minister of the gospel sometimes refuses to acknowledge, and who with the hem of the garment drawn for fear of de- filement,we pass by "on the other side."

The diouglit must have often occurred to many Runcornians that there were to be found men in our midst whose characteristics were eminently worth pourtraying for the benefit of others* Persons who wear the "pure flower of a blameless life," and persons whose souls had sunk to that "red hell "of shame and vice, that misery and despair have claimed them as their own peculiar victims. There are, unquestionably, men in the humblest village who stand as types of the great mass of people which constitutes humanity. It is our intention to give weekly a brief character sketch of those who claim the honour of being citizens of Runcorn. It will, at all events, be of local interest. The materials for our sketches will be the man in the street ; the man in the club; the man we have "eaten salt with" ; the man who pose as a leader; the man who is content to be led. Since we believe that in order to hold up the mirror of life faithfully, all sorts and conditions of men must come within its range, we shall not hesitate to choose the subject of our remarks from the lowest stratum of society,or ascend to the proudest monied coati the town can boast of. The sketches will be impartial yet critical,and every effort will be made to obtain correct information and verify the state- ments which may be made from time to time. The great object in commencing this series of sketches will be not so much to interest and amuse, but an endeavour to help onward the search of truth, more appropriately expressed by one of the greatest poets of the nineteenth century : . I hold it truth. That men may rise on stepping stories Of their dead selves to higher things.

If wa can stimulate anyonT3 to nnevvei ex:lrtion in the battle of lite, or cause a feeling of pride in our historic town, our effarts will be amply rvuid Article No 1 n,xt issue. RUNCO;IN NOTES,

LBY. A CORRESPONDENT.]

A glance at the report of the London and North- Western Railway Company leads one to believe that an effort will be ras,de to secure the sanction of Parliament to the widening of the celebrated Run- corn Bridge. All I can say is that I hope the sanction will be given, and that the bridge will be widened. But, at the same time, Ido hope that our District Council will so guard itself as to secure a lower toll for crossing from Widnes to Runcorn or vice versa. Don't let the idea of bringing money into the town stand in the way. For the money re- ceived by Runcorn and Widnes consequent upon its construction has been twice paid for in toll, and more than twice carried out of the town.

C3nsiderable interest is at present manifested in the disease—tuberculosis and an animated dEscus- sion on the subject took place at the Rural Coun- cil meeting on Tuesday. Mr. Woodward expressed considerable contempt at the conplaints which had been laid before that body respecting the disease and ridiculed the idea of milk from pure stock be- ing contaminated. That would be all right if it were so. But.there is perhaps more in the jocular remark of Mr. Knowles "that possibly it was due t) the rid- ing and driving the milk got en route",than appears at first sight? Of one fact we can be pretty cer- tain, notwithstanding the aspersion upon the " scientists of the present day" that the samples tested at Liverpool showed unmistakeable signs of serious affection from tuberculosis, but there might have been some slight mistake as to the source that supply came from, brought about by the "rid- ing and driving ?" Whatever the cause, the offence was pretty clear; and farmers instead of getting up in arms should be only too pleased to have dan- gers which they, in the hurry and bustle of their life cannot observe, pointed ouG t) them. They seem to have a difficulty in looking at the matter dispassionately.

The decision of the Guardians to allow parents to se their pauper children, at the discre,tiou of the Niaster of the [louse simply leaves the matter where it was r_befora, and is as it should be. The master ought to have the power te ue hi i discretion iR such matters, and all who know the master of the Dutton Workhouse have nothing to fear on that count. If he were denied that privilege, where would the decipline of the house be? Echo answers where?

The success of the Million Guinea Fund protnoted by the Wesleyans, so far as Runcorn is concerned, has been! somewhat phenomenal and already the projected subscription of 1,000 guineas, has been promised, and the figures are rapidly appro iching the figure so ardently advocated by the Rev.C.W.L. Christien (1,500 guineas.) His was no vain boast when he said that "if that figure was not reached he did not know tha giving quali- ties of his own circuit." Enthusiastic meetings have been held at Halton, Weston, and Westoa Point, and still there is Camden to COMO. We under- stand that" The Can:Lien's are coming," and won't be behind.

TUBERCULOSIS IN CATTLE. THE MARKERS I7P IN ARMS.

At a meetine of the Parliamentary Committee held at the Clerk's office Runcorn, on Friday the 10th met, the Clerk reported the result of his, and Mr. Swinton' in- terview with the Town Clerk of Warrington, with refer- ence to the WarrMgton Cerperatioe Bill, and after full consideration thereof, it was resolved that certain clauses be required for the protection of the District Council. The committee considered a letter from the Rural Dis- trict Council of Bucklow, enclosing copy of clause 20 of the Manchester Corporation (General Powers) Bill 1899, with r ference to the sale of the milk of any cow which was suffering from a diseased or indurated udder. It was resolved to adopt the views of the Bueklow Council, , viz : That when the subject was considered ripe enough to be dea't with, a bill more complete, and ap- plying to the whele country should be introdeced into Parliement by the Government, should be supported by invoking the assistance of the local members of Parlia- ment, and the County Council to have the clause elimi- nated from the bill. Dr. Adams said he had visited one of the farms which supplied the milk complained of as affected with tuber- culosis. He found one cow with an indurated udder, and when he had made that discoiery the occupier de. alined to let him go any further. Whether he had any more cows in a like condition it was not for him to say. The main thing to establish was that all farmers should be only too glad to have these things pointed out to them: He did not know how to advise the Council. They had no powers. (Hear, hear.) He could only recommend that every f armor, not only those interested as council- lors, but every farmer in the district, should ascertain simply primary facts. A cow with an indurated udder was such as to bo deleterious and ought to be eliminated from the stock. He was simply their Medical Officer and net their execetive officer, and he merely wished to advise them. ft was a question which was being presses to the point. He had had an interview with the County Medical Officer of Flealth with regard to the question as well as with re- gard to that ef the rivers pollution. He felt that if a spirit of compromise could be estabished in the district among all the tenant farmers whe had milch cows, and that such farmers would ascertain primarily if their cows had indurated udders or diseased teats, and have them certified as to whether they were affected with tu- berculosis or otherwise—if all that were done, he thought they would know exactly where they were. If taey would only do that volun- t atilt, it would save the Council and their sanitary effners no end of trouble. He was warned off one farm as ho had told them. He did not say that in any spirit of hostility to the farmer. He found the fifth cow affected with tuberculosis so far as the condition of the udder could enable him to ascertain it. Under the circumstances he did not feel justified in pressing his in- speetion any further. In contrast with the ease he wished to say that he visited another farm and found there was no cause for suspicion with reference to the cattle wi,le one exception, and that was a cow which had resentty celved. There wore 9 heal of cattle and no suspicion at all with regard to them. That farm was one reported from Liverpool. Mr. S. Woodward said it was reported to them that naiiksupplied to Liverpool from two farms in their dis- trict was affected with tuberculosis to a dangerous de- gree. The question of tuberculosis was being pushed ' ' unduly in the interest of the public health by medical offic-re. He did not think it was being pushed by their own Medical Officer, but it was being premed unduly by the County eledical Officers and others and he was sorry to see that Manchester and Liverpool wore propos- ing some very stringent measures with reference to it. Elis own opinion was that before anyone b lean thus to proceed they ought to be sure of their ground. It was a very serious matter to attack any interest. and it was a very serious matter to attack the interest of the fanners ai; the present time when everything f.eerned against them and the clouds were black and dark. Oa the authority of Mr. Walter Long, M.P., it was stated that this question of tuberculosis in milk and in cattle as affecting milk was still in a very in- definite and uncertain stage, and as President of the Board of e.gricalture he was not prepared to recommend to Parliament that they should take any steps towards the preparation of any measure that should apply to farmers in the protection of milk,or that should apply to farmers in reference to the destruction of tuberculosis cattle. Coming back to the two cases reported in their own district, Mr. Woodward said that one of the two farms was that of Mr. Faulkner, at Newton-by- Frodsham. Mc. They were proud of their history arid proud of their traditions, and could look back with pride upon the self-denial of those who had gone before them. (Applause.) That meeting was for them to decide what they would do towards the accemplish.nent of the fund. Be believed that they would be Emcee:still in getting money. He did not say that with any boasting. They did not wish to be boastful in the least, as some people would have there believe they were doing. They were going to raise the money because they considered it was necessary. He then dealt with the objects to which the fund is to be devoted. The Revs. C. W. 1,. Christian an I R. J. Clifton also spoke. The Rev. C. W. L. Chr.seen nfernd t , the fact that at first they intended to raise £1,530 ía St. Paul's Circuit, but subsequently it was decided to have £l,OOO. and go up to beyond that, welch he felt sure they would accomplish. (Applause.) 'Die Rev. C. Garrett (Liverpool) who on rising was warmly received, said is was a j oy to him to be present at that meeting. Of course it was one of those eve its which stood out in one's lifetime, and a solemn feeling would naturally settle over the minds of all of them. It was such a meeting they had never been at before, and might never be at again. They had never seen the begin- ning and close of a century, yet for once in a lifetime Some of them would be permitted to end one century, and naturally a very solemn feeling settled over their lives as they thought about it. While they were selomn at the dark evening they were ending, they were, he thought, all more or less under the influence of gratitude. The qua.tion had been put and he bad heard it again and again "Wed, What have we to be so thankful for ?" He he I a few words zo say about that and wanted them to look at what he would put before them ; to weigh it up; and then come to a resolution as the result of that weighing. Look at the last century. the 18th century, it was like Looking at the dark ages. Its leading features were brutality, tyranny, and ignorance—these three words described the 18th century, and they had but to turn over the pages of history to be convinced of it. What had they to be thinkful for? Ha wished that there were a greater number of working men present, as all through that movement his hope had been the people, for the broader they made the basis the firmer Was their superstructure. He would far rather have the matter done by a million than by a thousand. (dear, hear.) One hundred years ago what was the state of the country? Remember Europe was a battie field. There ev. re four million of men itadr arms. ill Europe were against us. Napoleon was , supreme, and his name was a " bogie man "by which I all the children old and young were frightened and sent to bed. Going back 100 years to that vary night, there were 27 thousand Preach prisoners in this coun:ry and they had to be kept as they best might. It was a dole- ful state of things, and the more they thought about it the more doleful it appeared. Look at the moral condi- tions. Think of a law beirag passed that no one shouNl bake bread, and both Houses of Parliament passed a law against the making of bread out of flour. What did workingmen enjoy in the good old times when George 111. was king? (Applause and laughter.) Work was scarce, wages low—farm lab ourers all over Eagland never thought of more than 6s. a week, and yet corn was £5 a quarter. That was in the good old times ; had they not then something to be thankful for. Then taxation ; talk of taxes they had nothing these 1 days compared with what they had 100 years ago. The bread was taxed ; the light was taxed. Peoele were tated for putting in windows, and if the window was , ealarged the tax was also enlarged with it. There were evidences of those times even at preceat in the blind windows of many old houses. Salt was taxed to 17 times its value; and then was not anything that you could, use that was not tweed. It- seemed that the only thing that men and women were made for in those days was to be taxed —(laughter)—and the legislation was for nothing but to make taxes. That was but 100 years ago and yet people asked what had they to be thankful for ? What had they not to be thankful for? Why they should rather say" Bless the Lord 0 my Soul, and all that is within me bless His Holy name." They were ignorant 100 years .ago ; half of their peaplo could not write or read a word, only about I could manage to write their own names, and judging by the specimens handed down to them, they were poor at Vest. (Laughter.) Books? there were no books to talk of. Everytody got drunk; if one bad a guest they made him drunk before they went te bed. "As drunk as a lord" was a general expression, ant it was counted the highest honour one could receive to bo drunker than anybody else. Then for evidence of the sensuality, of the filthy cenversation the men of thoss times indulged in, a trace of it was still left amongst them, for why did the ladies leave the room after dinner, but that the men might talk—such converiatioa that was unfit for their ears. Truly they were a miserable nation 100 years ago. Everybody swore ; the lawyer swore in court, ; arid tno ladies swore in the drawing room; the judge swore in the court and the parson in the pulpit. (Lehghter.) Tee, they were a miserable lot in those good old times. (Increased laughter.) What had they to be thankeil for?. Why thankful they did not live in those times. He toad not uttered one word that he could not give them in black and white. There was no speculation in what he had teen saying. Again, ',yak at the workpeople. He heard talk of going in for shorter hours; if they had been there 100 years ago they would have known what it was to werk all day. Little children were carried in the arms to the mills at five o'clock in the morning, and were left at tee doors. Young children were employed at such an early age that they were dying on every side. The popu- lation was fast decrealine. , Where did they replenish their stock from. The Worlhouses. They took people out of the workhouses in those days with a vengence but they were brought to a terrible end. It had been said that the law did net go below the soil. There was no law that went into the atollieries.The coal was dragged by little children on all fours. There was nothing but cruelty to children in those days. The poor little chimney sweep. Many of them could remember how the black curtain was hung up and the little fellow sent up the flues, and if he stuck, why the fire was kindled underneath, and the little fellow had to go up the flue. But thank God those things were ended, that time was gone, and like a horrid nightmare it had passel away. Dealiag with the political aspect of those times, the speaker, said they had laws, but they were all made by the rich, and the poor were only regarded as poor dumb and deaf cattle. They were all made on the principle, and he hoped the young men present would bear it in mind that the poor then had duties bet no zigtits, whilst the rich had rights but no duties. That being the state of things it was little wonder that laws were broken, and when anyone offended against the law no solicitor or advocate was provi&d, The Press wal gagged. one editor of a Cambridge paper, who dared to find fault with the law that allowed 1,000 strokes of the "cat" to be given sailors for petty offences,. who dared to cry shame because one poor sailor had died. at the 750th etroke, was tieed £lOO for writing his ar;ocie and sent to prison for 6 months. Such was the state of things in Eagland at that time, that there were 227 capital offeaces committed in the one year, all punish- able with hanging, and all for such offences as eiecthig young, trees, shearing sheep ; injury to Westminster Bridges; stealing cloth worth 55. It was something horrible to think of. One of the judges of those nays had said " It was useless to try and reform criminals, the. only thine, they could do was Co hang them." And they did hang them, and so what with dying like rotten sheep, and hanging them wholesale, the population was becoming so reduced as to make the authorities afraid to take a census for fear the French should know bow few they were. (Leugh.er.) Look at the religious condition. Why Dr. Johnson had said that he never knew but one religious person in all his life, and that was John Wesley—(applause)—but he was in snch a hurry at the time that he could not talk to him. On Monday afternoon, at ..irlestown, a van belonging to Mr. W. Monks, grocer, of Warrington, cams into violent collision with a pair horse lurry belonging to the Midland Railway Company. It appears that John Halton, Mr. Monks' carter, was proceeding to the Sankey Sugar Company's Works when the Midland lurry came down the lane at a gallop, there being only room sufficient for one cart, and Halton seeing the difficulty of the position backed his van against the wall, but was unable to avoid the collision. The force of the impact was very great, the van being ver; much damaged, and the horse injured, bat not sufficiently to prevent it from being walked home. Tile lurry sustained no damage.

He applied for a warrant for the apprehension of the man, and he was arrested that morning. He should call Sergeant Evans, who would say that the prisoner was " nothing but a druoken lazy scoundrel." He could find work with a c litractor, but*he wou'd not do anything.—Mrs. Newton said that during the past f,)tir or five months her husband had left her and the children almost in a state of destitution.—Sergeant Evans deposA that the prisoner was a habitual drunkard. ALLEGED CRUELTY TO A HORSE AT WESTON.—Peter Noon, earth-aware dealer, Runcorn, was summoned for alleged cruelty to a pony at Weston on the 4th of February.—De`en4- ant pleaded not guilty.—From the evidence of Ezra Darling- ton, Lightburn-street. (in the employ of the Runcorn Co- operative Society), William Dunbavin, Old Hall Farm and Police Constable Wylie, it appeared that the horse fell down in the vicinity of the Co-operative Stores, at Weston. I injured its knees and scratched itself, and was in their opinion unfit for work.—Defendant admitted thst the horse had fallen, and had its knees injured, but sub- mitted that the wounds were nothing but superficial. THE PARR HA.ldi S,iTUILDA.Y EVENING

CONCERTS.

The last but one of the series of Saturday evening concerts took place on Saturday. The attendance was only mediocre, which may be attributed to the deterioration in the quality of the concerts whici has been noticeable this last few weeks. Mr. J. G. liewson sang "So, Sir Page,'‘ in a capable manner, but he was better appreciated in "Ohl Oh ! hear the wild wind blow," and had the pleasure of a recall. 'Come, sweet morning," and "A day dream," were renderei by Miss Ethel Panton in excellent manner and for her rendering of " Rest of the story "an encore was demanded. A humorous duet by Messrs W. and F. Benson was a pleasing item, and the Ben- son Brothers later appeared in a whistling trio and gave some fine imitations of a military band, Ger- man band, and a drum and fife band for which they were well applauded. The same artistes also gave some pantomimic "Pictures from daily life" for which-they were encored, and a laughable duet entitled "Pussy's courtship' which was a complete success. Miss Fanny Russell (da,nseuse) contributed a coon song and dance: "Lonesome Coon." The dance at least, was gracefully executed. A tam- borine Adance and "speciality skirt dance" were also given by Miss Russell and for the latter she was deservedly recalled. Miss Alice Richardson possesses a rich contralto voice and her rendering of "Sunshine and rain" was pleasing, but the singer appeared to better ad- vantage in" Dear little shamrock and in answer to an encore Miss Richardson sang " Comin' thro' the rye " Gay tom-tit" was also sung by Miss Richardson in charming style and resulted in an enthusiastic encore. Mr. Alfred Ainsworth accom- panied most efficiently.

SERIOUS CHARGE AGAINST A WA R RINGTON MAN.

George Norman, of 16, Cairo-street, Warrington, was charged at the Manchester City Police Court, on Saturday, before Mr. T. Parkinson, with falsely representing himself to be a detective to a female.—On Thursday he went to a house in Hester-street, Greenheys. Whilst there he represented himself to b 3 a detective.He also went to the house on Friday and demacded 103., threatening to re- port the house as a disorderly house. He was then given into custody of Police Constable Bloomfield.—Mr. Harris, who defended the prisoner.said he only represented him- self to be a detective for the purpose of beating a hasty retreat from the house, and that he did not demand any money, lo:rt refused some that was offered him. He had plenty of money in his possession.—Mr. Parkinson des- cribed the case as a very bad one, and the pris)ner was fined £lO and costs in each of the two cases, with the alternative of two months' imprisonment.—Notim of appeal was given.

To the Edkor.,of the Warrington " Examiner."

Deir Sir,—With respect to the cas3 of Mr. George Norman, concerning whom reports have appeared in several newspapers, will you he so good as Co permit me, through your columns, to ask your readers and the public generally, to suspend their judgmmt until after the appeal has ben heard. _

The derails of the case having been fully related to me, and having known Mr. Norman intimately for nearly seven years, I have every reason for believing that he will lo! able to eitablish his complete innocence of the grave charges brought against him. While eNquiring for a person with whom ho had business to transact, Mr. Norman was invited to enter a certain house, after entering which he had reason to be- lieve it was of a disreputable character,ani immediately endeavound to leave the house, the door of which had been locked, and on the spur of the moment be then stated he wal a detactive (an untruth which he acknew- ledges and is sorry for.) Except on this one point, my own confidence ii his moral uprightness and integrity of character is unshaken.—Yours faithfully,

EDWARD T. REED.

17, Museum-street, Warrington, 14th Feoruary, 1899.

IMPUDENT THEFT AT WARRINGTON.

At the Liverpool Assizes on Tuesday, before Mr Justice Day, Thomas Troy, aged twenty-eight, bar- ber, was charged with having at Warrington, on the 16th January, stolen two boots and one pair of trousers, the property of James Leslie. He pleaded not guilty. Mr. Rigby Swift prosecuted, and called evidence to show that the pro,ecutor kept a second hand clothes shop:at 39, Pinner's-brow, Warrington. On the 16th ult prisoner went into the shop and asded Mrs. Leslie to show him a shirt. Mrs. Leslie refused. Whilst her back was turned she heard a noise in the shop, and turning round saw the pri- sonar making for the door. He mu down the street. She followed him into the yard of a public-house. She there saw him putting on a pair of boots which he had token from the shop, whilst on his arm were a pair of trousers. A policeman came up and ar- rested the prisoner, who when charged said, "Well, all I have got to say is I did not take them." Pri- soner was found guilty. His Lordship said prisoner had a very bad character, lie wee sent to gaol for a mouth.,

JOHN G.AiILICK AGAIN. DARING ESCAPE FROSI STAFFORD

GAOL.

What must be regarded as a carefully-planned and distinctly diver escape from prisou took place early on Kaiday morning at Stafford. Among the prisoners in custody was one John Garlick, alias John Grant, dime John Dixon, alias John Robert flinry, who was under- going five years' penal servitu ;e for fraud. He was a transfer prisoner from Walton Gaol. Garlick formerly carried on business at L7thgoe's-lane Warrington, and ior dafrauding several People was sen- tenced to 12 months imprisonment at Liverpool Quarter Sessions, and later, for long firm frauds at Ashton-in- Makerfield was sentenced by Mr. Justic Paillimore tO five years' _imprismment.

The" BirMinghaus Gazzitte " gives the following ac- count of Garlick's escape from gaol :—Aiter a lapse of ten or ehven years, during which there have teen no suscessful attempts to obtain a short-lived liberty, Staf- ferd Prison was on Monday the scene of an escape which will take some beaing in regard to daring and audacity if the information to hand cu be relied upon. The man who so succes,siully esceiced from the cwtody of his eanlers is John Garlick, but lie is a.so known in police circles as Dixon, Grant and Henry. He is a joiner by trade, aid is thirty-niee years of ;tee. He was a transfer penal servitude prisoner from Liverpoll, where he re- ceived a sentence of five years for fraud. It seems that whilst in prison he was employed at his own trade, and he was locat!cl in a cell which was fitted up as a work- shop. Inetead of following his employment the full number of hours, he appears to haw dew:God some por- tion of his time to the task of boring a hole in the wall of the building, and that so successfully as to escape detection. It goes without saying that while, airing himself within the predate of the prison he sat veyed the premises, and decided upsn a plan of cam- paign. That plan was to reach the too of the boundary wall, whice is some six or seven yards high, and then disappear on the other side at a less frequented pare. To accomplish this he prepared some lenetas of wood as a ladder in his cell, and, splicing them, managed by climb- ing, to attain the summt of his ambitioa as well as that of the wall. This done the rest appears to have been Wain sailing, and he let himself down the outside of the wall, dropping into the asylum grounds. The rest gave him little trouble, and •he made for the open country acrose the asylum grounds.. It may be reilily under- stood that not a little consternation prevailed •-ithin the prison when it was discovered that the convict was miss- ing. Information was immeniatelt given to the police, who put forth every effsrt to bring the wanderer back, and, by acquainting the pile in adjoining towns, to form a cordon so as to intercept the convict wherever he might appear. His identiy, of course, wonld not bs difficult to establish, as he was attired in the regulation clothes with the exception of the coat, provided, of course, the man did not succeed in getting rid of the convict garb before his arrest was effeeted.

VW Sir,—The main point at issue between Mr. Fairctough and the Corporation is the question of the suitability of the Bold Water for domestic or trade purposes. lat once admit that the water is organically pure, as pure in fact as the water from Winwick but the Corporation are advised that the Bold water is too hard to be sup- plied to their customers without treatment. DEATH OF MIL R. NORMAN.

It is with deep regret that we have to record the death of gr. R. Norman, which took place at his residence 55. Greenway-road, on Saturdsy night after an unueually short illness. Deceased had been in failing health for some time, having never com- pletely recovered from a severe illness, whic in- capacitated him from manual labour, several years ago. His friends little thought, however, that the end was so near. The early part of the week saw him in his usual healtb.and on the Sunday previous to his decease he worshipped, as was his custom, at St. Peals Wesleyan Chapel. On Tuesday he was confined to his bed, and the alarming symptoms in- creasing it was deemed advisable to call in Dr. Rob- inson. The most strenuous efforts were made to prolong his life but all was' in vain; and it was apparent on Saturday afternoon that he was rapidly sinking. The worst fears were entertained and re- alised, the deceased gentleman passing away with- out having regained consciousness. Mr. R. Norman's career was a remarkable one in every respect. Born in Weston he became converted at that village at the age of 14, and from that time devoted himself to the cause of Christianity with earnestness and vigour. He became eventually a "local," and enjoyed a reputation as a powerful preacher among the village districts. His services were in request in towns beyond Frodsham, Ditton, Kingsley, etc., and for many months he preached the gospel as a paid evangelist. His work was "owned of God" by the many conversions which attended his services. And more than one of his converts has joined the body of Wesleyan itinerant preadhers. In later years he followed the business of coal merchant, and travelled in connection with this calling, over the greater part of England and Wales. He had been a member of the Wesleyan body over 50 years. He took a most prominent part in building the Methodist Chapel at Weston, and he was appronriatly called "The Father of Run- "ern Methodism." He established, and for many years held the position of leader of the Halton-road Society Class which was the largest in the district, and which was the parent of many other smaller society classes. Conversions Were net at all uncom- men at these weakly meetings. He enjoyed the honour of being the oldest local preacher of the circuit, and though latterly unable to follow these religious duties, ha still employed his spare time in visiting the sick members of his church. He was a staunch supporter of the !Jiberal party, and was a well informed man on all secular matters. The Methodist connection and the town generally have received a severe blow. Those who knew him best esteemed him best. His constant aim was to leave the world better than he found it. Re leaves a widow to mourn his loss. gees*. F. J. and A. R. Norman are his nephews, and the late honoured Thomas Norman was his elder brother. He had reached the mature age of 69.

THE FUNERAL,

The funeral toell place on Wednesday afternoon, amidst manifestations oil profound regret. Blinds were drawn, and other mark i of respect were exhibited on all sides. The remains were taken to the St. Paul's Church, where a short, memorial service was held, the officiating ministers being the R 393 C W L Christien and R J Clif:on. After the ()peeing porti in of the burial service had boen concluded and the singing of the hymn " What are these arrayed in whits," the Rev C W L Chriatien said he was not about to give al a idreis with special reference to the life and work of their dear de- parted frietid. as br the request of the family it was in- tended that should be given on Sunday. Yet he felt he would but be expressine the sentiment of ev6rfone there present as well as the sentiment of the great number who could not be preceet, when he said that the loss of their dear friend was great. They had parted with one of the trueat friends of their charcn. A man of sterling worth arid a ma.' who had prove I that worth in a lone aril consiF- tent life before the eye of everyone. Ole who hid laboared amoigst others in the work of Christ, tett by a scroke of action his labours were now ended. Mr. Norman was one whose life gave to him a great wiefo!- ness—usefuln-ss in being al-,!e to win others. nor WAS he coniimel to one plav for from his life there etreamed up rays of influence which were felt a Lon; way off, so that none co Ild estimate the result of his life's latrurs in ties cause Gd—only God Himself. They had r arted with one who bad left a noble example behind him, one woo seueht to make the bet of his life for the benefit of others. He was best love I by those who kne x him toes', and his memory would be an inspiration to others to fol- low in aHa of work for Cerist. Mr. Christirn felt ho was but expressing the feelings of all when he said how deeply they all sympathised with her, who for 50 sum- mere vralke.l besides him ; ani who hid encouraged and enahled him that wee na more to so faithfully de his work. He concluded by inviting all to unite in prayer for the bereaved :;idow and family.

Afv.r the sintYing of hymn 938 "Ther3 is a Lind of Pure Diligh." Mr. G ,wyer, orffanisc, played tha "Dad March__" in Saul on tie orgai.

There is some talk of refunding, but if there is to be any allowance at all, it will have tl be general, as the people will never agree to the large consumers alone reaping the benefit. It will have to be an all-round allowance, or none at all.

POLICE COURT AND PRISON GATE MISSION.

There are s-Yeral encouraging features in the work of the Mission for the year just close 1. 2,733 visits have been made to 26 police and petty se:- sional courts during which an nggregate of 13477 persons were spoken to by the Missi-maries, in the cells and courts, or an increase of 3,532. As a proof of the mlllence ot the magi3trates in the work they enlisted the help of the Missionaries in 1,010 CiSeS last year, many of them first off-nders, RS against 589 in the previous year. 7.667 breakfastS were given to prisoners on di charge at the Mission Room, near Walton Gaol,or al increase of 1043. The number of total abstinence pledges taken in conni-c- Mon with the Mission was 3.199 as against 2,682 in the previous year. The Firewood Factory at Bootle gsve employment to 256 m'en or an increase of 94. 2,226 letters were written by Missionaries with re- ference to cases. The principid items in the work for. the past month ere as follows: --During 226 visits to the courts 1,393 persons werre conversed with in the courts and cells of whom 647 were afterwards visited in their abodes, 160 persons were attended to in the courts at the desire of the jus- tiees, -22 lads mostly runaways were taken to Homes or sent back to friends. Four women were taken to the Society s Temperance Home and 21 others to refuges or restored to friends. Clothing was furnished to 36 cases. generelly to enable them to accept employment. 556 breakfasts were given to discharged prisoners at the Walton Mission Room. 280 pledges were tskon &e. INDUSTRIAL PROGRESS IN WARRINGTON. NEW WORKS.

OPENING OF MESSRS. FLENFIER RUSSELT.,

AND COMPANY'S WORKS.

Thera was a strong element of progress in the opening of the new works of Messrs Fletcher Russell and Co., Limited, near Wildersponl Causeway, on Wednesday afternoon, and one which bids fair to make Warrington still more important as a manu- facturing centre. Many in Warrington will re- member the small beginnings of this firm and will have watceed with interest its Uninterrupted pro- gress. Six and a half years ago there was an amal, gamation of the firms of Messrs. Thomas Fletcher and Company and Messrs. W. and A. C. Russell and Company of Manchester, and henceforth they became known as Messrs. Fletcher Russell and ComDany,Linaited, gas engineers and artistic ironfounders. The firm have realised for some time pa=t that their premises in Thynne-street were quite inadequate to cope with the growing demand of their businese, and at was in order to meet this deeelopsnent, that the new works have been erected. At Warrington the work carried on has been chiefly confined to fitting together and finishing the various appliances, the work of casting being carried out by the Pendleton department. In the new premises a large portion of this work will be done, and this will greatly facilitate the work. It would be im- possible here to enumerate the appliances manufac- tured by the firm, but they may be briefly divided into six sections, three connected with the War- rington department, and three with the Manchester department. In the first, place there are domestic labour saving .appliances such as cooking, water heating, laun- dry appliances, boiler burners, coffee roasters, gas governors, &c. The second section include 3 gas fires, stoves, greenhouse beilemand general heating apparatus. Then in the third place may be men- tioned laboratory and workshop appliances for gas, gsseline,petroleum and oil gas: furnaces of all kinds blowpip el, blowers, plugabers' stoves, boilers, Ste. The before mentioned are connected with the War- rieeton department. At Manchester the work may ale) be divided into three sections. One comprises trial tels, overmentels, grates, restaurant tables, garden chairs, curb fen lets, &e., another, kitchen ranges for coal, or gas and coal combined,coal cases, doors and frames, waslahouse boilers, a r brick ',and the third, lavatory stands and brackets. Tee prenaises cover an area of 121 ac e; and have been erected and fitted uo at acoit of £59,000, in- cluding the perehase of the sits. There are four b'ocks of buildings and in their erection no par- ticular style of architecture has been followed, the arrangement of the various; depertmenta and their utility having been kept uppermost in the minds of these responsible for the haild.ings. T P), and able assistance has been rendered by Mr. William RNeil Theoffi3es front Wilderspool Causeweyland are 126 ft. long by 63ft. broad. They co-aprise three storeys, the ground tber boil:Le uti- lized for storeenoms, the second floor for offices and the third floor for showrooms. Immediately behind is anether three-storey building 'which will be used as a warehouse, a grinding shop, and for annealing, enamelling and gas testing. In close , proximity there is also a boiler house with econo- miser and an engine house fitted us with electrical machinery, providing electric light and driving power.throughout the premises. The moulding shop eemprises another block of build- jogs, 181 feet long and 196 feet wide, divided into five sectional 'roofs. It is pro- vided ,with two cupolas supplied by Messrs. Thwsites. of Bradford, which are used in smelting the ore. There is another block to be mentioned and that is the one running parallel with the foot- ball field, which is 172 feet by 771 feet, and is to be used as a machine fitting shop. The works are connected with the London and North Western Railway by n siding running alongside the foundry and boiler hens°. Mr. C. W. Davenport has been the builder, and Hr. Broanhurst, clerk of the works. Th- number of employes at present is 400, but it is estimated that in a short time the number will reach 600. -

DEATH OF MR. ARTHUR SHAWCROSS.

We regret to stste thst the death took place at a quarter-past ten on Sunday morning, at his resi- dence in Sankey-street, of Mr. Arthur Shawcross, who had been in business in Warrington for nine years eg a veterinary surgeon. The deceased. who was 33 years of age, contracted a chill a few days ago. and influenza and pneumonia setting in he suceumbed. His medical attendants were Drs. Braille and Josenb.

COUNTY OF CHESTER. WILD BIRDS PROTECTION ACTS, 1880, 1894, AND 1896. NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the following is a copy of an ORDER which has been made by the Right Honourable Sir Matthew White Ridlee, Baronet, M P., Secretary of State for the Home Department, viz : WILD BIRDS (ENGLAND), Coaster) The Wild Birds Protec-ien (County of 18i18. Order, 1898, Dated 15th F bruary, In pureuance of the powers conferred Liven me by the Wild Bird's Protection Acts, 1880 to 1896, and upon application by the County Council of Chester, I hereby make the'following Order,which shall apply to the whole of the County of Chester :- 1. This Order may be cited as "The Wild Birds Pro- tection (County of Chester) Order, 1898." 2. The time during whieh the killing and taking of wild birds, or any of them, is prohibited by the Wild Birds Protection Act, 1880 (43 and 44 Vic., cap. 35),5ha1l be extended in the County of Chester) so as ta be from the Ist day of Match to the 12th day of August in each year. 3. The Act of 1880, as by Section 2of this Order, shall apply within the whole of the County of Chester to the Kestrel and Heron as if those species were included in the Schedule of that Act. 4. The taking or kfiling of the following species of birds, namely :—Kingfisher, Great Crested Grebe,Heron, Goldfinch and Owl, shall be pi ohibited within the whole of the County of Chester during the whole of that period of the year to which the protection of wild birds under the Act of 1880 (as extended by Section 2 of this Order) does not extend. 5. The taking or deqroving of the Eggs of the Owl, the Biitern, thelKingfisher, the Kestrel. the Heron, the Sheldrake, the Dunlin,the Blackheaded Gull,the Common Tern, and the Oyster Catcher, eh dl be prohiblted in the ! whole of the County of Chester. ! And I make the following further Order, which shall apply within the portion of the Hundred of Wirral coloured pink on the maps deposited in the Home Office ! and in the office of the County Council, and embrac- ing the low-lying lands and sandhille situate between the estuaries of the Rivers Dee and Mersey, of which the boundaries shall commence to the south at a point of the main highway of the County of Chester 200 yards to the west of the Dock Cottages near the " Docks" Railway Station of the Wirral Railway, run along the main highway to Bidston Villace, and then turn in a northerly direction and run to Bidston Railway Station of the site Railway, then turn in a westerly direction and run along toe Wirral Rallivay until it reaches the Meols Station of such rail- way, then run along the highway near to the Meols Railway Station in a northerly direction to the Irish Sea then en the north along the margin of high-water mark of the Irish Sea to a point at New Brighton where the Yellow Noses join the said Irish Sea, and then run in a southerly direction to" Warren Drive "or road ,and ! along" Warren Drive" to the most northerly house and grounds on the west side of such drive, then along the western boundary of the houses and premises situate along the western side of such drive or road to a point 100 yards or so from Grove-road then turning in a westerly direction along the northern boundaries of the houses and premises in Grove-road, Wallasey, until it reaches the Wirral Railway, then turning in a southerly direction along the Wirral Railway until it reaches the Wallasey Marsh. then along the edge of the marsh in a southerly direction until it reaches the Poulton Half- penny Bridge, then turning in a westerly direction on the edge of the said marsh, until it reaches along the edge of tke marsh the main highway leading from Poul- ton to Meols, such parts within the aforesaid boundaries as are market garden lands and are rot coloured in the said maps are excepted from the operation of this Order. 6. The Twenty-fifth Anniversary. COMMENTS OF THE PRESS. A large company of gentlemen joerueyel frem Liverpool-et. to Cambridge 12 July last, as the guests of Meesrss. S. Chivers and Sons, of Jam and Jelly tame, who invited them to visit their fields and factory in commemoration of the twenty-fifth anniversary of the celebratsd firm. Fine weather favoured the trip and the outing will be memorable am mg j nernalistic jeurneyings—and junketings. STRAWBERRIES AD LIB. A little while and the guests were let hese int, a strawberry field, with injunctions to pick and choose, till the field suggested the ravages of loenste. Wnen the surfeited raiders t ire themselves ruefully away finger-tips were pink with juicy fruit, like the bands of workers in Turkey-red. LUNCH AND SENTIMENT. In a new wing of the f 'dory lunch was served, and continued success pledged unreservedly to the firm of Chivers. Time senbr partner, in a few ulosteutatious sentiments, nerrated the history of the firm's ptenomenal prosperity as makers of preserve for the million. From the smallest beginning the firm had come to employ 800 hands. Other speakers testified in glowing terms ti the boon conferral upon the district by the great jam factory, which had praetically built up one of the happiest villages in Merry England. A glance at Hieten provel the truth of this. INSIDE THE FACTORY. Those of us who hitherto felt squeamieh abeut factory-made preeerves wil have no such scruples in future. Everything inside the factory was scrupulously neat and cle in—and this verdict includes the pretty girls. The wood tables were scrubbed to spotless whiteness., and the airiness and sweetness of the place were remarkable. A miniature railway system ant aqueous canals facilitated the moving about of the trucks of jams and jellies. It was a triumph in ingenuity and sanitation.—The "Sun," 13th July, 18S8. THE CHRISTIAN WORLD. " The factory, which we inspeccel at a comfortable interval alter luncheon, cannot be adequately, descritpd except by an expert. But an unskilled observer could not help noticing the brightness, clean- ness, and airiness of the buildings, and the heelrhy and content6d leek of the workers." THE RURAL WORLD. "We saw at Histon a first-class factory, fitted up with all modern requirements for turning out a first-class article. The employees, the machinery, and the utensils were the pink of cleanliness. In the factory everybody was clean and bu3y ; whilst in the strawberry fields were hundreds of busy workers from the villages around, which alone gives a good idea of the activity prevailing at this highly impor- tant country centre," FARM FIELD AND FIRESIDE. " It must be said that the materials employed were of firet-elass and exceptional quality; the straw- berries. for Instance, being the principal crop at the moment under treatment, being really equal to dessert fguit. Cleanliness was a striking feature. The girls and women, too, in the factory were tidy and clean, and with the airy and sweet surroundings which modern and large buildines provide, there was everything to induce confidence in tee exeellence of the Jams and Jellies turned out from here," EAST ANGLIAN DAILY TIMES. "What impressed visitors most were the scrupulous and even delicate ckaoliness of every operation, the great pains taken to ensure absolute purity in and Jellies, and the amicable relations that evidently exist between employers and ea:in:cued."

LATEST NE WS. DYNAMITE OUTRAGE IN THE BLACK. FOREST. At Blakeney, Forest of Dean, on Wednesday night, an explosion oecurrei at the residence of an old man named Davies, a big charge of dynamite having been placed under the kitchen window. The house was Welly dam- aged tsgether with its cont nots, and Davies sustained fractared ribs, but his wife escaped. A year ago an at- tempt was made to wreck the some house, and recently t) Vow up the saw mills in the neighbourhood. The outrage is considered to be an act of spite. THE SEARCH FOR ANDREE.‘ Professor Nathers will be ready to start in nearch of Andree in the ship Antartic by the beginning of June. His expediton is regarded as the last chance of rescuing Andre and his colleagues, supposing that they are still alive. The famous Danish explorer Brunn will join the expedition. Baron Nordenskjoelds states that he fully shares Nansen's views as to the amount of credence to be attached to the Krasaquirck story. YESTERDAY'S FOOTBALL. FINAL RESULT. Sheffie!cl United, 2. Rainy. THE NANTWICH CLERICAL SCANDAL. Mr. A. N. Hornbv presided on Wednesday night over a packed meeting of Nantwich churchmen and parish- ioners, called to obtain an expression of sympathy with the late curates in the stand they took previous to the recent inquiry into the conduct of the recter,et Chester. Mr. Boyer (urban councillor)ealled upon the parishioners to withhold their support from the church. Toe reso- lution was carried unanimously, and a fund for present- ing a testimonial to the curates, one of whom has been appointed to a London curacy, was also pansed. DEATH OF A FAMOUS ATHLETE. NEW YORK, THURSDAY. 14. E. Myers, ex-champion athlete runners who took part in many cont,sti in England, died suddenly yester- day from heart disease. He held the world's records for 50 yards, 500 yards, and 1,000 yards. RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS, The fortnightly meeting of the above was held at the Dutton Workhouse, on Tuefday. There were present. Mr. J. Woodward (in the clair). Rev. Dr. Gastaldi, Messrs. S. Taylor A R. Norman, J. Meadowcroft, T. Reynolds, with dr. Cr. F. Ashton. (deputy-clerk). PARENTS AND PAUPER CHILDREN. Admiral Parry moved that it be an in truction to the Master that in future the parents of children who are in the Workhouse be at all reasonable times allowed to see such children on their visiting the Workhouse for such porp-se whether such parants had previously deserted their children or not. Mr. Wallington seconded. . l'Ar. Norman thought the master should have some dis- cretion in the matter. Admiral Parry said he would add the words" Subject to the discretion of the Master." The resolution was then adopted. FINANCES. Mr. T. Wright submitted the Finance Committee's re. port: The paymeets that day amounted to £287 14s 8d; and included amongst other i'ems:—Outrelief, Runcorn £BO ; Frodsham, £24 ; Buciwnrth. £2l. Tne balenco in the treasurer's hands was £1,536 41 81.—Ou the motion of Mr. Wright, seconded by Mr. Chadwick, the report was adopted. THE ELECTION OF COUNCILLORS AND GUARDIANS.
